Builds the schema that basic facts GENERATE larger facts through place value structure.
This is the bridge between single-digit fluency and multi-digit multiplication.

```
Easy:   6 × 4 = ___     so:   6 × 40 = ___      60 × 4 = ___
Hard:   7 × 8 = ___     so:   7 × 80 = ___      70 × 80 = ___      7 × 800 = ___
```

**Format D — Fact Sprint (5 related facts in a cluster):**
Instead of 5 random facts, give a cluster with visible structure — the same factor,
or a fact family. Students notice patterns while practising.

```
Easy:   5 × 2 = ___      5 × 3 = ___      5 × 4 = ___
        5 × 5 = ___      5 × 6 = ___

Hard:   9 × 4 = ___      9 × 5 = ___      9 × 6 = ___
        9 × 7 = ___      9 × 8 = ___
        (Pattern: what do you notice about the digits in the tens and ones places?)
        I notice: ______________________________________
```

**Format E — Properties of Operations (named, applied, schema-building):**
*Why this format:* The identity, zero, and commutative properties reduce cognitive load
dramatically — but only when students can call on them deliberately. For ELL/SPED
students the zero property (n × 0 = 0, not n) is a persistent source of error.
Naming the property after applying it builds the schema that operations have
predictable, reliable behaviours — which is the foundation of algebraic thinking.

```
Easy:   Complete and name the property:
        8 × 1 = ___    Property used: _______________________________
        6 × 0 = ___    Property used: _______________________________
        5 × 4 = 4 × ___ Property used: _______________________________

Hard:   Use properties to simplify — show why you don't need to calculate:
        583 × 0 = ___     Because: ____________________________________
        47 × 1 = ___      Because: ____________________________________
        12 × 9 = 9 × ___  I'll calculate ___ × ___ instead. Easier because: ______
        99 + 0 + 1 = ___  Properties used: _________________ and _________________
```

**Format F — Commutative Flip (turn it to make it easier):**
Students rewrite the expression in the order that makes mental calculation easier,
then explain why that order helps. This makes the commutative property practical
rather than abstract — a thinking tool, not a rule to recite.

```
Easy:   Which order is easier? Rewrite and calculate:
        2 × 7:   easier as ___ × ___ = ___   (why? _______________)
        3 × 8:   easier as ___ × ___ = ___

Hard:   Rewrite and use a strategy:
        4 × 25:  ___ × ___ = ___   Strategy: _______________________
        3 × 24:  ___ × ___ = ___   Strategy: _______________________
```

---

**CATEGORY 4 — Number Bonds and Decomposition**

*Why this builds schema:* Number bonds encode the part-part-whole relationship that
underlies ALL four operations. The goal is flexibility — not knowing that 7 + 3 = 10,
but knowing that 10 can be any combination of two parts, and fluidly choosing the
most useful decomposition for any given calculation.

**Format A — Bond Diagram Completion:**
The standard bond diagram. Always vary WHICH part is missing — sometimes the whole
(top), sometimes one part (side). Vary this within a session.

```
Easy:        ( 15 )          Hard:        ( ___ )
            /       \                    /         \
          ( 6 )   ( ___ )            ( ___ )      ( 47 )
                                      = 100
```

**Format B — Show All the Ways (flexible decomposition):**
Students find multiple decompositions of the same number. The pedagogical power
is the constraint: they must find DIFFERENT ways, not just the first one they think of.
This builds the understanding that decomposition is not unique.

```
Easy:   Show 3 ways to make 16:
        16 = ___ + ___
        16 = ___ + ___
        16 = ___ + ___

Hard:   Show 4 ways to make 100:
        100 = ___ + ___
        100 = ___ + ___
        100 = ___ + ___ + ___     ← (this one uses three parts)
        100 = ___ + ___ + ___
```

**Format C — Make-a-Ten Bridge (strategy made visible):**
The format itself shows the strategy. Students fill in the intermediate steps —
they cannot skip to the answer without executing the strategy. This is overt
practice of a covert expert process.

```
Easy:   8 + 6 = 8 + ___ + ___ = 10 + ___ = ___
        9 + 7 = 9 + ___ + ___ = 10 + ___ = ___

Hard:   38 + 7 = 38 + ___ + ___ = 40 + ___ = ___
        47 + 26 = 47 + ___ + ___ = 50 + ___ = ___
```

**Format D — Bond to the Next Benchmark:**
Builds the "how far to the next friendly number" sense that underpins efficient
mental computation. Students must identify AND quantify the gap.

```
Easy:   370 + ___ = 400          85 + ___ = 100
Hard:   4,650 + ___ = 5,000      7,840 + ___ = 8,000
```

---

**CATEGORY 5 — Mental Math Strategies**

*Why this builds schema:* Mental math is not about clever tricks — it is about
understanding number relationships well enough to choose the most efficient route.
The format must show the STRATEGY, not just the answer. If a student can write the
answer without showing the strategy, the format is not testing what we want.

**Format A — Number Ladder (chain of operations, record each step):**
Each rung of the ladder is one operation. Students write the intermediate result
at each step. The format makes the chain of reasoning visible.

```
Easy:   Start: 12  →  ×2  →  ___  →  +10  →  ___  →  −7  →  ___

Hard:   Start: 50  →  ÷2  →  ___  →  ×3  →  ___  →  +25  →  ___  →  −18  →  ___
```

**Format B — Compensation Strategy (show the rounding AND the adjustment):**
The format forces students to record BOTH the rounded calculation AND the
adjustment. Students who don't understand compensation will skip the adjustment step
— this format makes that visible immediately.

```
Easy:   49 + 36:   Round up: 50 + 36 = ___   Adjust: ___ − ___ = ___

Hard:   198 + 47:  Round up: ___ + 47 = ___   Adjust: ___ − ___ = ___
        52 − 29:   Round up: 52 − ___ = ___   Adjust: ___ + ___ = ___
```

**Format C — Doubles and Near-Doubles Chain:**
Students extend from what they know (doubles are fast) to what they can derive
(near-doubles are one step away). The chain format makes the derivation explicit.

```
Easy:   I know:  6 + 6 = ___
        So:      6 + 7 = ___     (one more)
                 6 + 8 = ___     (two more)

Hard:   I know:  25 + 25 = ___
        So:      25 + 26 = ___
                 25 + 27 = ___
                 24 + 25 = ___   (one less)
```

**Format D — ×10 / ÷10 Structure:**
Builds the multiplicative understanding of the place value system.
Students see the pattern and complete it — the format is the lesson.

```
Easy:   6 × 1 = ___     6 × 10 = ___     6 × 100 = ___
Hard:   7 × 1 = ___     7 × 10 = ___     7 × 100 = ___     7 × 1,000 = ___
        840 ÷ 1 = ___   840 ÷ 10 = ___   840 ÷ 100 = ___
```

---

**CATEGORY 6 — Number Line Sense**

*Why this builds schema:* The number line is the most important visual model in
elementary mathematics because it encodes magnitude, direction, order, and distance
simultaneously. The key is to use OPEN number lines for estimation (endpoints only,
no tick marks) and MARKED number lines for exact placement. Both are needed.

**Format A — Place It (open number line, estimation):**
Students mark the approximate location of a number on an open number line.
The endpoints are given — students must reason about where between them the
number falls. No tick marks — this is magnitude estimation, not counting.

```
Easy:   Where does 37 go?
        |___________________________|
        0                          50
        Mark it with an arrow. Write the number.

Hard:   Where does 6,400 go?
        |___________________________|
        5,000                    8,000
        Mark it. What is it closest to — 6,000 or 7,000? ___
```

**Format B — Number Line Jumps (addition/subtraction as movement):**
A printed number line with two points marked. Students draw the jump and write
the equation the jump represents. This makes addition = move right, subtraction
= move left tangible rather than abstract.

```
Easy:      |-------|-------|-------|-------|
           0      10      20      30      40
        Mark: start at 12. Jump +15. Land at: ___
        Equation: 12 + 15 = ___

Hard:      |-------|-------|-------|-------|
           0     100     200     300     400
        Start at 340. Jump −80. Land at: ___
        Equation: 340 − 80 = ___
```

**Format C — Neighbours (nearest benchmark):**
Students name the benchmarks on either side of a given number and decide which
is closest. Builds magnitude sense and rounding readiness.

```
Easy:   52 is between ___ tens and ___ tens.
        It is closer to ___ tens.

Hard:   4,370 is between ___ hundreds and ___ hundreds.
        It is closer to ___ hundreds.
        It is between ___ thousands and ___ thousands.
        It is closer to ___ thousands.
```

**Format D — Distance Between Points:**
Students find how far apart two numbers are — the conceptual foundation for
subtraction, elapsed time, and measurement.

```
Easy:   |----|----|----|----|----|----|
        0   10   20   30   40   50   60
        How far from 15 to 45? ___ units   Equation: ___ − ___ = ___

Hard:   Distance from 380 to 650 = ___    Show your jump: ___ + ___ = ___
```

---

**CATEGORY 7 — Estimation and Reasonableness**

*Why this builds schema:* Estimation is not an approximation skill — it is a
verification skill. Its purpose is to catch errors before they are written down.
The formats below require students to commit to a range or a judgment BEFORE
computing, building the habit of estimation as a check, not an afterthought.
Avoid multiple choice — it removes the production demand that makes estimation meaningful.

**Format A — Bracket It (find the floor and ceiling):**
Students name the upper AND lower bound. This forces them to think about magnitude
from both directions simultaneously. It is the most rigorous estimation format.

```
Easy:   47 + 38 is between ___ and ___   My estimate: ___

Hard:   473 × 6 is between ___ and ___   My estimate: ___
        Explain how you knew: _________________________________
```

**Format B — Too Big, Too Small, or Just Right?**
A problem and a stated answer. Students write B (too big), S (too small), or ✓
(reasonable), then write their own estimate. This is NOT multiple choice — it is
a two-part judgment: classify first, then produce.

```
Easy:   28 + 43 = 81     Is this answer:  B / S / ✓ ?    A better estimate: ___

Hard:   317 × 4 = 908    Is this answer:  B / S / ✓ ?    A better estimate: ___
        Show why: 317 is close to ___, and ___ × 4 = ___
```

**Format C — Closer To? (benchmark comparison, written justification):**
Students do not circle — they write their answer and write ONE word or number
that shows their reasoning. This makes the mental process visible.

```
Easy:   Is 43 × 5 closer to 200 or 250?   Answer: ___
        I know because 43 is close to ___

Hard:   Is 68 × 9 closer to 540 or 630?   Answer: ___
        I know because: _____________________________________
```

**Format D — Number Line Estimate:**
Students mark where they think the answer falls on a printed number line.
This is a spatial production task — it externalizes magnitude reasoning.

```
Easy:   Estimate 48 + 33. Mark your estimate:
        |___________________________|
        0                         100

Hard:   Estimate 5 × 78. Mark your estimate:
        |___________________________|
        0                         500
        Nearest ten: ___
```

---

**CATEGORY 8 — Patterns and Algebraic Thinking**

*Why this builds schema:* Algebraic thinking is not about letters — it is about
generalisation. When students identify a rule, extend a pattern, or balance an
equation, they are building the schema that variables and functions will later
attach to. The equals sign must mean "same value on both sides" — not "write the
answer here." Formats that show BOTH sides of the equals sign build this.

**Format A — Rule, Pattern, Extension:**
Students name the rule (in words or symbols) and then extend the pattern.
Naming the rule is the essential step — without it, extending is guessing.

```
Easy:   Rule: _______________
        Pattern:  3, 7, 11, ___, ___, ___

Hard:   Rule: _______________
        Pattern:  2, 6, 18, ___, ___, ___
        What will the 8th term be? ___    How do you know? _______________
```

**Format B — Function Table (rule applied to multiple inputs):**
Given some input-output pairs, students find the rule and complete the table.
This is the foundational format for algebraic thinking — one rule, many instances.

```
Easy:   IN → OUT:    2 → 5     4 → 7     6 → ___     ___ → 13     10 → ___
        Rule: add ___

Hard:   IN → OUT:    3 → 12    5 → 20    8 → ___     ___ → 36     12 → ___
        Rule: ___________      Is 100 a possible output? ___ Explain: ___________
```

**Format C — Balance the Equation (equals = same as):**
Students find the value that makes both sides equal. The blank can be on
either side — this fights the "equals = answer" misconception directly.

```
Easy:   6 + ___ = 3 × 5           ___ − 4 = 2 × 6

Hard:   4 × 9 = ___ × 6          3 × ___ + 5 = 4 × 8
```

**Format D — Error Hunt (notice and correct):**
A number pattern with one deliberate mistake. Students circle the error,
write what it should be, and — for the Hard version — explain why.
Identifying errors builds deeper rule understanding than producing correct examples.

```
Easy:   5, 10, 15, 21, 25, 30 → Circle the mistake. It should be: ___

Hard:   3, 6, 12, 21, 48, 96 → Circle the mistake.
        It should be: ___     Explain: _________________________________
```

**Format E — Comparison of Expressions (< > = without fully computing):**
*Why this format:* This is the algebraic bridge. When students write < > = between
two expressions rather than between two numbers, they must reason about mathematical
structure — not just compute. "Is 3 × 8 the same as 4 × 7?" requires either computing
both or spotting a relationship. Both are valuable. For ELL/SPED students this format
also fights the "equals = answer" misconception by showing expressions on both sides.

```
Easy:   Write <, >, or = without calculating. Then explain:
        4 × 5  ___  2 × 10    Because: ________________________________
        30 − 8  ___  8 + 14    Because: ________________________________

Hard:   Write <, >, or = — use reasoning, not full calculation:
        5 × 12  ___  10 × 6    Because: ________________________________
        7 × 9   ___  63        Because: ________________________________
        4 × 13  ___  5 × 11    My estimate: ___ vs ___   Answer: ___
```

**Format F — Prove or Disprove (written justification, no multiple choice):**
A bold claim is made. Students write whether it is true or false AND write the
proof — a number sentence, a diagram, or a written explanation. This builds the
understanding that mathematical truth requires evidence, not just belief.

```
Easy:   True or false?  8 × 3 = 4 × 6
        Answer: ___     Proof: ________________________________

Hard:   True or false?  5 × 18 = 10 × 9
        Answer: ___     Proof: ________________________________
        
        True or false?  (3 + 4) × 2 = 3 × 2 + 4 × 2
        Answer: ___     Proof: ________________________________
        Name the property this shows: ___________________________
```

---

**CATEGORY 9 — Fractions, Decimals and Percentages (Grades 3–5)**

*Why this builds schema:* Fractions are where number sense most often breaks down
for ELL/SPED students because the symbolic notation is dense and counterintuitive
(larger denominator = smaller piece). Every format must make magnitude visible, not
just symbolic. The number line is the primary tool because it encodes size spatially.

**Format A — Fraction Number Line (magnitude made spatial):**
Students mark the fraction's location on a printed number line from 0 to 1.
The line must be divided into appropriate units. This makes "how big is this
fraction?" a spatial judgment, not a symbolic one.

```
Easy:   Mark ¾ on the number line:
        |----|----|----|----|
        0   ¼   ½   ¾    1
        Arrow ↑ at: ___

Hard:   Mark ⅝ on the number line:
        |--|--|--|--|--|--|--|--|
        0  ⅛  ¼  ⅜  ½  ⅝  ¾  ⅞  1
        Is ⅝ greater than or less than ½? ___    How do you know? ___________
```

**Format B — Equivalent Fraction Chain:**
Students extend the chain by multiplying numerator and denominator by the same
factor. The visual chain makes the multiplicative relationship — not the coincidence
— visible.

```
Easy:   ½  =  __/4  =  __/8  =  __/10

Hard:   ⅔  =  __/6  =  __/9  =  __/12  =  __/18
        All of these equal: ___ (as a decimal) and ___% (as a percentage)
```

**Format C — Compare and Order (no number line — magnitude reasoning):**
Students order three fractions and write the inequality chain. They must reason
about common denominators or benchmarks — the format gives them space to show
which strategy they used.

```
Easy:   Order from least to greatest:    ½    ¾    ¼
        ___ < ___ < ___    I compared using: _______________

Hard:   Order from least to greatest:    ⅔    ¾    7/12
        ___ < ___ < ___    I found a common denominator of: ___
```

**Format D — One Whole Bridge (fraction + complement = 1):**
Students find the fraction that completes the whole. This builds the "fractions
have a complement" schema that underlies fraction addition and subtraction.

```
Easy:   ¾ + ___ = 1          ⅝ + ___ = 1

Hard:   ¾ + ___ = 1½         ___ + ⅔ = 1          2⅓ + ___ = 3
```

**Format E — Decimal-Fraction-Percent Bridge:**
All three representations of the same value, side by side. Students complete
whichever is missing. The format makes the equivalence visible as a relationship.

```
Easy:   ½  =  0.___  =  ____%        ¼  =  0.___  =  ____%

Hard:   ___  =  0.75  =  ____%       ⅜  =  0.___  =  ____%
        0.2  =  ___  =  ____%
```

---

**CATEGORY 10 — Subitizing and Quantity Recognition**

*Why this builds schema:* Subitizing is the ability to recognize quantity without
counting. It is the foundation of number sense because it builds the "how many is
this?" feeling that makes estimation and magnitude judgments intuitive. The part-part
format extends subitizing into additive decomposition simultaneously.

All formats are printed on P1. No flash cards needed. Students see the image
and write — the image is printed; the response is written.

**Format A — Ten-Frame Reading (quantity + distance from 10):**
Students write what they see AND what is missing. This builds the "how far from 10"
sense that underpins addition and subtraction of single-digit numbers.

```
Easy:   [Ten-frame printed: 7 dots filled]
        How many? ___     How many empty? ___     How far from 10? ___

Hard:   [Ten-frame printed: 8 dots filled in an irregular pattern — not standard left-to-right]
        How many? ___     How many to make 10? ___     Write the bond: 8 + ___ = 10
```

**Format B — Part-Part Ten-Frame (decomposition + subitizing combined):**
Two colours in the ten-frame. Students write the parts AND the sum.
This builds fact fluency from quantity recognition simultaneously.

```
Easy:   [Ten-frame: 4 solid + 3 hollow counters]
        Red: ___     Blue: ___     Total: ___     Bond: ___ + ___ = ___

Hard:   [Double ten-frame: top frame has 9 dots; bottom frame has 6 dots]
        Top: ___     Bottom: ___     Total: ___
        Write the equation: ___ + ___ = ___
        Write the near-double it connects to: I know ___ + ___ = ___
```

**Format C — Dot Arrangement (irregular, not dice-pattern):**
Dots in a non-standard arrangement. Students must recognise sub-groups and
add them — this is the foundation of flexible decomposition.

```
Easy:   [6 dots: 3 arranged as a triangle, 3 arranged in a row below]
        Groups I see: ___ + ___     Total: ___

Hard:   [13 dots: irregular clusters of 5, 4, 4]
        Groups I see: ___ + ___ + ___     Total: ___
        Another way to see it: ___ + ___
```

---

**CATEGORY 11 — Word Problems and Problem-Solving Warm-Ups**

*Why this builds schema:* Word problems build the schema that mathematics describes
reality — and that the student's job is to extract the mathematical structure from
a real situation. The most powerful format is not solving a problem but WRITING one,
because it requires the student to understand the relationship the operation represents.

**Format A — Mystery Number (clue-based logical deduction):**
Clues narrow the possibilities progressively. Students write the number AND the
equation or reasoning that confirms it. This builds logical constraint satisfaction —
a foundational thinking skill.

```
Easy:   I am a 2-digit number.
        I am between 30 and 40.
        I am even.
        My digits add to 7.
        I am: ___     Proof: ___________________________

Hard:   I am a 3-digit number.
        I am less than 500.
        I am a multiple of both 6 and 9.
        My hundreds digit is 4.
        I am: ___     Proof: ___________________________
```

**Format B — Write the Story (equation → real situation):**
Students see an equation and write a 2-sentence word problem that matches it.
This forces schema activation: what situation does this operation represent?

```
Easy:   Equation:  24 ÷ 4 = ___
        Write a word problem for this equation:
        _________________________________________________
        _________________________________________________
        Answer: ___

Hard:   Equation:  3 × ___ = 54
        Write a word problem where the unknown is what you are finding:
        _________________________________________________
        _________________________________________________
        Answer: ___
```

**Format C — Error Analysis (spot, name, fix):**
A solved problem with one deliberate mathematical error. Students circle the
mistake, name what type of error it is, and write the correct solution.
This is harder than solving correctly because it requires understanding what
went wrong, not just what the right answer is.

```
Easy:   Problem:  Tom has 48 stickers. He shares them equally with 5 friends.
        Solution shown:  48 ÷ 5 = 9 remainder 2
        Mia says each friend gets 9 stickers.
        Is Mia right? ___    What did she miss? _____________________
        Correct answer: _______________

Hard:   Problem:  A box holds 24 pencils. A school orders 15 boxes.
        Solution shown:  15 + 24 = 39 pencils.
        Circle the mistake. What operation should it be? ___
        Correct solution: _______________
```

---

**CATEGORY 12 — Measurement and Data Sense (Spiral Review)**

*Why this builds schema:* Measurement requires units, magnitude, and conversion — all
of which connect directly to place value and multiplication. The goal is not converting
units mechanically but understanding the SIZE relationship between units.

**Format A — Clock Reading and Elapsed Time:**
Always include BOTH the clock image and elapsed time — reading a clock and
reasoning about elapsed time are different cognitive tasks.

```
Easy:   [Printed analog clock showing 2:45]
        Time shown: ___:___
        What time is 20 minutes later? ___:___

Hard:   Start time: 10:35 AM     End time: 12:10 PM
        Elapsed time: ___ hours ___ minutes
        Show your jump on the number line below:
        |------|------|------|
        10:35  11:00  11:35  12:10
        Jump 1: ___ min    Jump 2: ___ hour    Jump 3: ___ min
```

**Format B — Conversion Pairs (unit relationship, not just procedure):**
Students fill in the conversion AND write the relationship that explains it.
This prevents the "just multiply by something" approach.

```
Easy:   1 meter = ___ centimetres
        2 meters = ___ centimetres
        5 meters = ___ centimetres
        Rule: to convert m → cm, I _____________________

Hard:   1 kg = ___ grams
        2.5 kg = ___ grams
        ___ kg = 3,500 grams
        Rule: to convert kg → g, I _____________________
```

**Format C — Graph or Tally Read (printed mini graph, one question):**
A simple tally chart or bar graph is printed. Students read ONE value and
then answer ONE question about relationships (more than, total, difference).
Keep data simple — the math should be accessible, the reasoning should not be.

```
Easy:   [Tally chart: Cats: ||||  Dogs: ||||  Birds: ||]
        How many dogs? ___
        How many more cats than birds? ___

Hard:   [Bar graph: heights of 4 plants, scale 0–30 cm]
        Which plant is tallest? ___     Shortest? ___
        Difference between tallest and shortest: ___ cm
        Total height of all 4 plants: ___ cm
```

---

**CATEGORY 13 — Multiplicative Structure (Arrays, Groups-Of, Area Model)**

*Why this builds schema:* Fast fact fluency (Category 3) can be achieved without
understanding what multiplication means. Category 13 addresses the conceptual layer:
multiplication is equal groups, it is rows-and-columns, it is area. For ELL/SPED
students, the jump from "6 × 4 = 24" (symbolic) to "6 groups of 4 objects" to "a
rectangle 6 units by 4 units" is not automatic. This schema must be built explicitly.
Without it, word problems involving multiplication fail not because students can't
calculate but because they can't recognise which operation the situation requires.

**Format A — Draw the Array:**
Given a multiplication expression, students draw the array (dots in rows and columns)
and label the rows, columns, and total. Drawing forces the visual schema into working
memory alongside the symbol — dual coding at its most direct.

```
Easy:   Draw an array for 3 × 4:
        [blank box — rows × columns]
        ___ rows of ___ = ___

Hard:   Draw two different arrays that both equal 12:
        Array 1: [blank]  ___ × ___ = 12
        Array 2: [blank]  ___ × ___ = 12
        What does this tell you about 12? _______________________
```

**Format B — Groups-Of (multiplicative language building):**
Students write the multiplication sentence AND a groups-of sentence for the same
situation. This builds the language bridge ELL students need between the operation
and its real-world meaning.

```
Easy:   [Image: 4 bags, each with 3 apples]
        ___ groups of ___ = ___    Multiplication: ___ × ___ = ___

Hard:   Write a groups-of sentence AND a multiplication sentence:
        5 × 8:   ___ groups of ___ = ___   ___ × ___ = ___
        Now draw it: [blank box]
        Turn it around: ___ groups of ___ = ___ (same answer? ___)
```

**Format C — Area Model (multiplication as rectangle):**
Given dimensions, students complete the area model by labelling and filling in
the total. This is the foundational visual for the standard multiplication algorithm
and for fraction multiplication in later grades.

```
Easy:   [Rectangle printed: width = 4, height = 3, interior blank]
        Label the sides.    Total area = ___ square units.
        Multiplication sentence: ___ × ___ = ___

Hard:   [Rectangle: width = 12, height = 6 — split with a line at 10]
        Left section: 10 × 6 = ___    Right section: 2 × 6 = ___
        Total: ___ + ___ = ___    So 12 × 6 = ___
        (This shows how the area model splits hard facts into friendly parts.)
```

**Format D — What's the Expression? (situation → symbol):**
Students read or see a real situation and write the multiplication expression.
This is the reverse of solving — it builds the schema of which operation represents
which real-world structure.

```
Easy:   6 children. Each child has 5 crayons.
        Groups: ___    In each group: ___    Expression: ___ × ___

Hard:   A garden has 8 rows of vegetables. Each row has 12 plants.
        Expression: ___ × ___   = ___
        Draw the rectangle: [blank]
        Write a division question this garden could also answer: _______________
```

---

**CATEGORY 14 — Factors, Multiples and Divisibility**

*Why this builds schema:* Understanding factors and multiples is not a standalone skill —
it is the lens through which multiplication structure becomes visible. When a student sees
24 and thinks "that's 4 × 6, also 3 × 8, also 2 × 12," they have a rich multiplicative
schema for that number. Divisibility rules are metacognitive shortcuts that only make sense
when students understand why they work. Prime vs. composite is a categorisation skill
that reveals the building-block nature of all numbers.

**Format A — Factor Pair Hunt (systematic, structured):**
Students list ALL factor pairs, starting from 1 and working inward. The systematic
search is the point — it builds the habit of checking every possible factor, not just
the obvious ones.

```
Easy:   List all factor pairs of 12:
        1 × ___ = 12
        2 × ___ = 12
        3 × ___ = 12
        Are there more? ___ factors total.

Hard:   List all factor pairs of 36:
        1 × ___    2 × ___    3 × ___    4 × ___    6 × ___
        Is 36 prime or composite? ___
        How do you know? ___________________________
        Which factor pair gives the most equal split? ___
```

**Format B — Prime or Composite? (prove it):**
Students classify and justify. The proof is essential — it forces students to actually
find a factor (or show that none exist), not just guess or remember.

```
Easy:   Is 15 prime or composite?    Answer: ___
        Proof (write a factor pair other than 1 × 15): ___ × ___ = 15

Hard:   Is 37 prime or composite?    Answer: ___
        Show your checking:
        37 ÷ 2 = ___  (whole number? ___)
        37 ÷ 3 = ___  (whole number? ___)
        37 ÷ 5 = ___  (whole number? ___)
        37 ÷ 7 = ___  (whole number? ___)
        Why do you only need to check up to about 7? ___________________
```

**Format C — Multiples Check and Generation:**
Students write multiples AND check whether a given number is a multiple.
The "is ___ a multiple of ___?" question requires a different operation from
listing multiples — it requires testing divisibility.

```
Easy:   Write the first 5 multiples of 6:
        ___ , ___ , ___ , ___ , ___
        Is 42 a multiple of 6?  ___   How do you know? ___________________

Hard:   Is 84 a multiple of 7?  ___   Is 95 a multiple of 5? ___
        Is 144 a multiple of both 6 AND 9?  ___
        Show: 144 ÷ 6 = ___   144 ÷ 9 = ___   Both whole numbers? ___
```

**Format D — Divisibility Rules (understand, don't just apply):**
Students apply a divisibility rule AND explain why it works at a basic level.
The goal is not to memorise rules but to understand that divisibility is about
even sharing — which connects to the groups-of schema in Category 13.

```
Easy:   Without dividing, circle all numbers divisible by 2:
        14    27    38    53    60
        Rule for divisible by 2: ___________________________________

Hard:   Without dividing, circle all numbers divisible by 9:
        27    36    42    54    63    81
        Rule: add the digits. If the sum is divisible by 9, the number is too.
        Check: 252 → digits add to ___ → divisible by 9? ___
        Check: 348 → digits add to ___ → divisible by 9? ___
```

---

**CATEGORY 15 — Mathematical Vocabulary in Context (ELL-Specific)**

*Why this builds schema:* This category does not appear in any standard number sense
framework — it is designed specifically for ELL students 1–3 years behind in English
academic language. Mathematical vocabulary is not just word knowledge; it is schema
knowledge. The word "quotient" does not just mean "the answer to a division question" —
it signals that division has happened, that equal groups were found, and that the result
has a specific relationship to the dividend and divisor. Practising vocabulary in
mathematical sentences — not definitions, not matching, not multiple choice — builds the
full semantic schema the word belongs to.

**Format A — Fill the Term in a True Mathematical Sentence:**
Students complete a sentence that requires both knowing the term AND producing a
true number example. This is harder than defining the term and more useful for ELL
students because it requires the term to function inside a mathematical idea.

```
Easy:   The ___________ of 6 and 4 is 10.        (term: sum / difference / product)
        The ___________ of 10 and 3 is 7.
        Write your own true sentence using the word "difference": ________________

Hard:   When I divide 56 by 8, the ___________ is 7 and the ___________ is 0.
        A ___________ of 24 is 6, because 6 divides into 24 evenly.
        A ___________ of 6 is 24, because 24 = 6 × 4.
        Use "quotient" in a sentence that includes a true division equation: _____
```

**Format B — Operation → Term (action to vocabulary):**
Students see what was done and name the result using the correct term. This builds
the link between operation and vocabulary in the direction ELL students most need:
"I did this action — what is this thing called?"

```
Easy:   I added 7 and 5.   The answer is called the ___________.   It equals ___.
        I subtracted 9 from 15.   The answer is called the ___________.  It equals ___.

Hard:   I multiplied 8 × 6.   The answer is the ___________.     It equals ___.
        I divided 48 ÷ 6.     The answer is the ___________.     It equals ___.
                              The number I divided is the ___________.
                              The number I divided by is the ___________.
```

**Format C — Vocabulary Bridge (same concept, two words):**
Students see two related vocabulary words and write what connects them — making
the semantic relationship explicit. This builds the vocabulary network, not isolated
word knowledge.

```
Easy:   "factor" and "product" — how are they connected?
        ___ × ___ = ___    The factors are ___ and ___.   The product is ___.

Hard:   "multiple" and "factor" — what is the relationship?
        If ___ is a factor of 24, then 24 is a ___________ of ___.
        Give an example using 6: ___________________________________

        "sum" and "addend" — complete:
        ___ + ___ = ___     The addends are ___ and ___.    The sum is ___.
```

**Format D — Word Wall Sentence (use it or lose it):**
Students write one original mathematical sentence that uses the target vocabulary
word correctly AND contains a true number equation. This is the highest-demand
format because it requires full production with no scaffold — it is what fluency
looks like.

```
Easy:   Use the word "difference" in a true sentence with numbers:
        ___________________________________________________________

Hard:   Use each word in one true mathematical sentence:
        "factor":   _______________________________________________
        "multiple": _______________________________________________
        "quotient": _______________________________________________
```

*Target vocabulary rotation across the year — select 1–2 terms per session:*
sum, addend, difference, minuend, subtrahend, product, factor, multiple, quotient,
dividend, divisor, remainder, equivalent, benchmark, composite, prime, expression,
equation, inequality, variable (Grade 5), exponent (Grade 5).

---

### Number Sense Question Generation Rules

**Category selection per lesson — MIXED, not single:**
Select 5 problem types from across the 15 categories, mixing them within the session.
Aim for variety: no two consecutive questions from the same category.

**Example mix A (lower grades):**
- Q1: Counting sequence (Category 1)
- Q2: Fast fact — addition family (Category 3)
- Q3: Part-part ten-frame (Category 10)
- Q4: Number bond to 10 (Category 4)
- Q5: Mathematical vocabulary — sum/difference (Category 15)

**Example mix B (middle grades):**
- Q1: Place value — expand it (Category 2)
- Q2: Factor pair hunt (Category 14)
- Q3: Estimation — bracket it (Category 7)
- Q4: Missing factor (Category 3)
- Q5: Draw the array (Category 13)

**Example mix C (upper grades):**
- Q1: Fraction number line (Category 9)
- Q2: Comparison of expressions (Category 8)
- Q3: Mental math — compensation (Category 5)
- Q4: Prime or composite — prove it (Category 14)
- Q5: Vocabulary bridge — factor/multiple (Category 15)

Track which categories have been used recently. Ensure all 15 categories appear over a
3–4 week rotation. Never skip a category for more than 5 consecutive sessions.

**Mandatory: every session includes a counting sequence.**
Every Number Sense Warm-Up must include at least one question from Category 1
(Counting Patterns and Sequences). This is non-negotiable — counting sequences are
the most accessible entry point for ELL/SPED students and activate the number line
schema before the rest of the lesson. The counting sequence is always Q1 in the set.
Vary the format and interval each session: skip count by 7s, count back by 25s,
fill a sequence starting at a non-zero number, count by fractions, etc.

**Priority guidance for ELL/SPED students:** Categories 15 (Vocabulary), 13 (Multiplicative
Structure), and 10 (Subitizing) are the three most commonly under-taught for this population.
These should appear at least once per week in the mix. Category 15 in particular should appear
2–3 times per week until the core vocabulary (sum, difference, product, quotient, factor,
multiple) is fully automatic.

**Sequence and counting format — the primary written format:**
Sequences are the most common Number Sense question type. They are ALWAYS written.
The teacher may also have students say the numbers aloud (choral), but writing is
always simultaneous or immediately after. Two sequence formats:

**Format A — Fill the sequence (blanks mixed with given numbers):**
```
Start at 255. Count by 5s. Fill in the blanks:
255 → ___ → 265 → ___ → ___ → 280 → ___
```
Include both provided numbers AND blanks. Ratio: roughly half given, half blank.
This prevents students from pattern-matching without understanding the rule.

**Format B — Write the next N numbers from a starting point:**
```
Start at 48. Count by 7s. Write the next 6 numbers:
48 → ___ → ___ → ___ → ___ → ___ → ___
```
All blanks after the seed number. Used when the rule is more challenging
and students need to apply it fully without a visual scaffold.

Choose Format A for younger grades or harder skip values.
Choose Format B for simpler skip values or when assessing pure fluency.

**Written format for every category type:**

| Category | Student writes… | Space needed |
|----------|----------------|-------------|
| 1 — Counting/Sequences | Numbers in blanks (Format A or B above) | Blank-per-number row |
| 2 — Place Value | Expanded form, digit value, rounding reasoning | Answer line or fill-in |
| 3 — Fast Facts | Answers, property names, or strategy shown in steps | Answer blank; property line |
| 4 — Number Bonds | Completes the bond diagram or writes the missing part | Bond diagram with blank |
| 5 — Mental Math | Each chain step and final result | Step blanks + final answer line |
| 6 — Number Line | Marks a point, writes the number, or writes distance | Printed number line |
| 7 — Estimation | Written estimate + written justification (no multiple choice) | Estimate blank + reason line |
| 8 — Patterns/Algebra | Rule, next terms, missing value, or proof | Rule blank + term blanks |
| 9 — Fractions/Decimals | Equivalent, comparison, converted form, or fraction chain | Answer line or comparison box |
| 10 — Subitizing | Writes quantity, parts, and bond from printed image | Answer blanks below printed image |
| 11 — Word Problem | Equation, answer, and/or proof | Equation line + answer line |
| 12 — Measurement/Data | Converted unit, time, or graph reading + one relationship | Answer line |
| 13 — Multiplicative Structure | Array drawn, expression written, area model completed | Blank box for drawing + equation line |
| 14 — Factors/Multiples | Factor pairs listed, prime/composite justified, divisibility checked | Structured fill-in |
| 15 — Math Vocabulary | Term written in a true mathematical sentence with numbers | Sentence line |

**Category 10 printed format:** A dot arrangement, ten-frame, or double ten-frame is printed
directly on P1. Students write the quantity, the parts (if two colours), and the bond.
The Hard version uses a double ten-frame or irregular grouping requiring sub-group counting.

**Category 13 printed format:** For array drawing, provide a blank box with light grid dots
(like dot paper) so students can draw rows and columns accurately without freehand guessing.
For area model, print the rectangle outline with dimensions labelled — students fill the interior.

**Category 15 note:** Category 15 questions have no numbers pre-given — students must produce
both the term AND a true number example. This is the highest-production-demand format in the
bank and should typically appear as Q5 in a set so students are warmed up before tackling it.

**Easy vs. Hard number complexity:**

| Grade taught | Easy (Option A — 2–4 grades below) | Hard (Option B — 1–2 grades below) |
|-------------|-------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|
| K | Counts within 5; quantities to 5 | Counts within 10; quantities to 10 |
| Grade 1 | Within 10; 2-digit numbers | Within 20; 2-digit operations |
| Grade 2 | Within 20; 2-digit place value | Within 100; 3-digit place value |
| Grade 3 | 2-digit operations; unit fractions | 3-digit operations; simple fractions |
| Grade 4 | 3-digit; simple fractions/decimals | 4-digit; fractions and decimals |
| Grade 5 | 4-digit; fractions; basic percentages | 5-digit; decimals; percentages |

**Format variety rule:** Rotate question types across sessions so no single format
(e.g., sequence completion) appears more than twice in a single 5-question set.
Across a week, students should encounter at least 6 different question formats.

**Pupil page for Section 2:** P1 Number sense (§9.2). Band A over band B, five rows each, every answer in
a slot. No estimation multiple choice (V20's Q4 "A: B: C: Circle" line is deleted; it contradicted this
bank's own "avoid multiple choice" rule).

---

## Fade levels (★ as teacher labels) (MANDATORY)

Stars are a **teacher** label for the fade level. **Pupil pages print no stars**; the band label names the
level [AUD C-14, INK-7]. **Owner default (2026-10-16, recommended; may change) — Q7:** no stars on pupil
pages. Flip: print a ★ glyph after the band label (still black, 15 pt).

| Teacher label | Pupil band | What the pupil sees |
|--------|------|-----------|
| **★** Supported | `Guided Practice:` | The first step of cell 1 printed in grey (trace it); hint scaffolds in grey in every Guided cell; the Steps list beside the cells. Every pupil starts here. |
| **★★** Independent | `Independent Practice:` / Practice pages | Hint scaffold on the first cell only (or none); structural scaffolds stay. The main learning goal: every pupil must reach this. |
| **★★★** Challenge | The last row of the Practice page | The lesson's edge cases (for example doubles, the big number second, an answer of 10) in the SAME cell format. Cross-topic or "reversed" items appear only if the lesson taught them. Stretch pages are paused. |

Every problem in every section is labelled with its fade level on the teacher pages (T3–T5) and on the
answer key routing page (T6), never on pupil pages.

---

## Lesson Time Budget (MANDATORY — 60 Minutes)

The timing prints on teacher pages only [P-LC-14].

| Section | Component | Pupil page | Time |
|---------|-----------|-----------|------|
| 1 | **Prerequisite Check** — 3–4 printed, routed questions | P0 | 4 min |
| 2 | **Number sense** — bands A and B | P1 (optional, default on) | 5 min |
| 3 | **Spaced retrieval** — 3 cross-domain questions on mini whiteboards | none (teacher page only) | 3 min |
| 4 | **Chaining Bridge** | none | 1 min |
| 5 | I Do — Silent Teacher + Self-Explanation | P2 Anchor chart (board copy) | 12 min |
| 6 | We Do — Guided Practice | P3 Lesson sheet, `Guided Practice:` band | 8 min |
| 7 | Check Question (board + mini whiteboards, never printed) | none | 3 min |
| 8 | You Do — Independent Practice + Word Problem | P3 `Independent Practice:`, P4 Practice ×n, P5 | 15 min |
| 9 | Plenary — Exit slip + Consolidation | P6 Exit slip | 7 min |
| — | Transitions | | 2 min |
| **Total** | | | **60 min** |

**Owner default (2026-10-16, recommended; may change) — Q3:** the printed, routed Prerequisite Check
replaces the V20 warm-up and the mini-whiteboard prerequisite check; spaced retrieval moves to mini
whiteboards. Flip: restore V20 Sections 1a/1b/2 (5 + 5 + 3 min) and drop P0.

> Note: The Number sense section is not interleaved retrieval — it is focused fluency
> and number sense practice drawn from the rotating bank. It runs before the cross-domain
> spaced retrieval. You Do is 15 min (V20's "20 minutes" header is corrected to 15 to keep the
> 60-minute total). For schools with longer periods, You Do can expand back to 20 min.

---

### Chunked Flow — Use When the Routine Table Has 4+ Steps Tagged New

**Chunked-flow trigger (the one definition, used everywhere):** count the rows of the T2 Routine table (Section 2) whose Tag is `New`. **4 or more → Chunked; 3 or fewer → Standard.** T1 prints the result with the count, e.g. `Flow: Standard (3 new routine steps)` (self-check E21b).

**First ask whether the routine should be two lessons.** One new thing per step [PED P-1]: a procedure
with 4 or more new routine steps is usually two lessons (for example a decide-only or notate-only lesson first,
then the full routine) [PED P-9]. Propose the split to the teacher. Use Chunked Flow only when the
teacher keeps one lesson [AUD C-20].

When the Routine Atomisation table (T2 Section 2) has four or more rows tagged `New`
and the teacher keeps one lesson, a single 12-minute I Do creates too much to
hold in working memory at once. Switch the lesson to Chunked Flow. Total time is identical — only the
structure changes.

| Section | Component | Time |
|---------|-----------|------|
| 1 | Prerequisite Check | 4 min |
| 2 | Number sense | 5 min |
| 3 | Spaced retrieval (mini whiteboards) | 3 min |
| 4 | Chaining Bridge | 1 min |
| 5a | **I Do Part 1** — first half of routine (Steps 1–[half of total]) | 6 min |
| 6a | **We Do Part 1** — practise atoms taught so far | 4 min |
| 5b | **I Do Part 2** — second half of routine | 6 min |
| 6b | **We Do Part 2** — practise full routine | 4 min |
| 7 | Check Question | 3 min |
| 8 | You Do — Independent Practice + Word Problem | 15 min |
| 9 | Plenary — Exit slip + Consolidation | 7 min |
| — | Transitions | 2 min |
| **Total** | | **60 min** |

**Trigger rule:** as stated at the top of this section (rows tagged `New` in the Routine table: 4 or more →
propose two lessons; if the teacher keeps one, Chunked Flow). State the flow and the count on T1 and at the
top of T3.

**Chunked Flow and optional enhancements:** If using Chunked Flow, limit to one optional
enhancement maximum (not two), as the flow change itself costs structural flexibility.

| Enhancement | Cost | Trade |
|-------------|------|-------|
| WAGOLL / Success Criteria | +3–4 min | We Do 8→5 min |
| Variation Theory Expansion Set (oral; pupil set paused) | +2–3 min | Replace 2 You Do problems |
| Marzano Multi-Encounter Vocabulary | +3–4 min | Prerequisite −2 min + We Do −1 min |
| Responsive Scaffolding (Expertise Reversal) | 0 min | No cost — circulation change only |
| Structured Academic Controversy (★★★, oral) | +4–5 min | You Do −2–3 problems |

---
## Part 8 — Print design: the MathQuest lesson profile (MANDATORY)

Every printed page — pupil and teacher — follows this part. It restates the site's lesson profile
(audit D1–D20) as values. The copy-ready stylesheet that implements it is Appendix A; the drawing recipes
are Appendix D; the self-check is Appendix E. **Fill the page with work, not with bigger print.**

### 8.1 Paper and page geometry [WDS 2.1, PG-1…6, D1]

**Owner default (2026-10-16, recommended; may change) — Q2:** A4 by default; US Letter only when the
teacher asks. Portrait. Print at 100 % ("no fit-to-page").

| Token | A4 (default) | US Letter |
|---|---|---|
| Sheet | 210 × 297 mm | 215.9 × 279.4 mm |
| Margins top / side / bottom | 12 / 12 / 14 mm | 12 / 14.95 / 14 mm |
| Live area | 186 × 271 mm | 186 × 253.4 mm |
| Header, page 1 (max) | 26 mm | 26 mm |
| Header, lifted (no Name/Date/Score: anchor chart, teacher pages) | 17 mm | 17 mm |
| Header, continuation pages | 12 mm | 12 mm |
| Footer band | 6 mm, outside the body | 6 mm |
| Body-to-footer gap | 3 mm | 3 mm |
| **Body height** = live − header − 6 − 3 | **236 mm** (full header); **245 mm** (lifted); **250 mm** (continuation) | 218 / 227 / 232 mm |
| Instruction line | 9 mm | 9 mm |
| Cell grid under one instruction, full header | 227 mm | 209 mm |

Rules:
- **PG-1** The live width is 186 mm on both papers; Letter widens its side margins. Every width rule in
  this file is written for 186 mm and is paper-independent.
- **PG-2** Every row count is computed from the body height, never fixed.
- **PG-3** The footer never grows. The header never exceeds 26 mm on page 1 and is 12 mm on continuations.
- **PG-5** Landscape is used only for the 2-up Exit slip (two half-pages side by side, a dashed cut line
  between them): A4 landscape, margins 12 / 13 / 12 mm (top / sides / bottom), live area 271 × 186 mm. Each
  half is laid out natively at **(271 − 6) / 2 = 132.5 mm** wide, with 3 mm clear on each side of the cut line;
  the footer and © line are shared by the sheet. A half's title may wrap to two lines (HD-14).
- `@page { size: A4; margin: 0 }` is set once and each page is a fixed 210 × 297 mm box with the margins as
  padding (Appendix A §3). Never add extra page padding on top of the margins (V20's double margin).

### 8.2 Font: Andika [TY-1…7, D3]

Every character on every page (pupil, teacher, SVG text) is **Andika**, weights **400 and 700 only**.
There is no italic and no 500 / 600: set `font-synthesis: none` so the browser never fakes one. Turn on
`font-feature-settings: "cv04" 1` (the open-top 4 that young pupils write) and
`font-variant-numeric: lining-nums tabular-nums` (equal-width digits that line up in columns).

**How to obtain Andika (SIL Open Font License 1.1).** Andika is made by SIL International and is free to
use, embed and redistribute under the OFL.
1. Download the release from SIL: `https://software.sil.org/andika/download/` (or the GitHub releases of
   `silnrsi/font-andika`). The zip contains `Andika-Regular`, `Andika-Bold` (and italics you do not need)
   as TTF and, in its `web/` folder, as WOFF2.
2. Keep `OFL.txt` with the font files whenever you redistribute them.
3. **Never load Andika from Google Fonts.** Google's copy is subset and strips the character variants,
   so the open 4 (`cv04`) is lost.
4. If you only have TTF, convert to WOFF2 (for example `python -m fontTools.ttLib.woff2 compress
   Andika-Regular.ttf`), or use the TTF directly with `format("truetype")`.

**Which release.** Andika **6.200** (SIL, 2023). The two web fonts this pack was built and checked with:
`Andika-Regular.woff2` (275,908 bytes, SHA-256 `bf3ee702ddfa18e3338ddb7b9943d534216b66f87fd884afb74f19e9da7dbada`)
and `Andika-Bold.woff2` (274,976 bytes, SHA-256 `a043f07c66d5cf0891b52d69692bb1ca4de981566bfd669305d31e776f23fc1e`).
A different build is acceptable if its GSUB table lists the feature `cv04` (test: in a browser,
`"4"` drawn with `font-feature-settings:"cv04" 1` has an open top).

**How to load it (self-hosted, no third-party request).** Either (a) put `Andika-Regular.woff2` and
`Andika-Bold.woff2` in a folder next to the HTML and point `FONT_URL` in Appendix A §1 at it, or (b) for a
single-file pack, embed each file as a base64 data URL:
`src: url("data:font/woff2;base64,<base64 of the file>") format("woff2");`. Use `font-display: block` so
the PDF is never printed in a fallback face. `local("Andika")` is tried first, so a machine with Andika
installed needs no file at all.

**Fallback stack (exact):** `font-family: "Andika", sans-serif;` The fallback is deliberately plain so a
failed load is visible; self-check E2 then fails and the pack must not be issued. Never substitute
another "friendly" face (Comic Sans, Century Gothic, OpenDyslexic): their digit shapes and widths differ
and every size in this file is calibrated on Andika. If Andika truly cannot be obtained, say so to the
teacher and output the pack marked "Draft — not in Andika".

**Glyphs.** True operators: `+`, `−` (U+2212, never a hyphen), `×` (U+00D7, never the letter x), `÷`,
`=`, `<`, `>`. Fractions are always stacked (numerator over a 1.5 pt bar over denominator), never
written with a slash. No KaTeX, MathJax or any maths library: Andika HTML + SVG draws everything.

### 8.3 Type sizes — one size L for the whole pack [WDS 3.2, LR-10, D2]

Every page of a lesson pack prints at size **L**. Grade does not change type size (grade changes only
counts and number ranges, §16). Nothing a pupil reads is below 11 pt.

**Pupil pages:**

| Element | Size | Weight | Line height | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Working digits (operands, equations, printed targets, answers on keys) | **28 pt** | 400 (key answers 700) | 1.15 (stacks 1.0) | The largest element on a pupil page |
| Fraction digits (numerator, denominator) | 20 pt | 400 | 1.05 | Whole part of a mixed number stays 28 pt |
| Digital clock digits | 42 pt | 700 | — | |
| "I Can" title | 18 pt | 700 | 1 line | Never more than 1.3 × the instruction |
| Instruction line; band label | 15 pt | 400; label 700 | 1.3 | |
| Text the pupil must read to act (story, steps, frames, vocabulary words, routine panel) | 15 pt | 400 | 1.3 (story 1.45) | The Steps box beside Guided cells may use 13 pt |
| Zone labels (T O heads, "1 less" / "1 more", unit captions, axis labels) | 12 pt | 700 heads, 400 otherwise | — | Names a zone; never an instruction |
| Step numeral in its accent circle | 12 pt (18 pt on the anchor chart) | 700 | — | Circle 6.4 mm (10 mm on the chart) |
| Number-tab numeral (Daily look) | 11 pt | 700 | — | White on a black 6 mm tab |
| Quiet letter label `a.` | 10 pt | 400 | 1 | The only pupil text below 11 pt, with the tab |
| Strand tab text | 10 pt | lines 1–2: 700, line 3: 400 | 1.12 | |
| Footer | 7 pt | 400 (page number 700) | 1 | Teacher-facing |
| © line | 6.5 pt | 400 | 1 | In the bottom margin |

**Teacher pages (T-TYPE):**

| Element | Size | Weight | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Title (same I Can title) | 18 pt | 700 | Shared with every pupil page |
| Section heads, e.g. `Section 5 · I Do (12 min)` | 15 pt | 700 | In an 8 mm band strip under a 2.25 pt rule; sentence case |
| Sub-heads | 13 pt | 700 | |
| Body | 11 pt | 400 | Line height 1.3 |
| Spoken lines (`Say:` / `Ask:` text) | 13 pt | 400 | Inside curly quotes “…” |
| Script labels `Do:` `Say:` `Ask:` `Note:` | 11 pt | 700 | In a 14 mm label column |
| Table text | 10 pt | 400 (head row 700) | The floor on teacher pages |
| Footer / © | 7 / 6.5 pt | 400 | |

### 8.4 Ink — five paints and their uses [INK-1…7, INK-30, INK-31, D4–D6]

| Token | Value | Allowed on | Never |
|---|---|---|---|
| `--ink` | **#000000** | All text, all lines, solid counters, number tabs | — |
| `--paper` | **#FFFFFF** | Page and cell backgrounds, hollow shapes | Tinted backgrounds |
| `--grey` | **#949494** (the only grey) | Shaded parts (fractions, bars), trace / model digits and rings, faded (Guided) hint scaffolds, model hops | Words a pupil must read; borders of structure; zebra rows |
| `--accent` | **#5B2A86** purple | Step numerals, their circles and the step icons, on lesson pages and teacher step rows only. Each has a second cue (numeral, shape) | Anything else: titles, words, boxes, cells |
| `--key` | **#C2410C** reddish orange | Answers on answer keys (in the pupil's slot, Andika 700), and expected answers in teacher scripts and tables | Questions, printed numbers, structure, pupil pages |

No gradients, shadows, emoji, clip-art, pastel boxes or coloured text. Two sets in one drawing are told
apart by **solid vs hollow**, never by colour or by grey level. The key colour prints as a dark grey on a
photocopier (luma ≈ 98/255, darker than the trace grey) and the accent prints as grey 66.

### 8.5 Strokes — the closed set [INK-10, D7]

| Width | Name | Used for |
|---|---|---|
| **2.25 pt** | rule | The header rule under the title; the top rule of every band; the hour hand |
| **1.5 pt** | heavy | The outer frame of a cell grid; band borders; sum rules in stacks; fraction bars; story box; outlines of objects (shapes, ten-frame border, bars, number-line axis and whole ticks, clock rim, number bond whole box); step icons; Daily-look cell borders |
| **1 pt** | dot | Step circles; grey strokes (trace rings, Guided tiles); dotted lines (1 pt dots, 1.2 mm pitch) |
| **0.75 pt** | hair | I Can cell borders; every writing place (line, box, circle, check box, digit strip); ten-frame interior; part ticks; table rules on teacher pages; hops; empty counters |
| **0.5 pt** | fine | Inside a visual only: minute ticks, grid lines, the clock's inner ring |

### 8.6 Line meaning and corners [WDS 5, LS-1…8, SL-11, D8]

| Style | Meaning | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Solid | Structure and given information | Frames, cells, rules, given shapes |
| Dotted (1 pt round dots, 1.2 mm pitch) | A model: trace it or copy what it shows | Trace rings, model hops, dotted counters to trace |
| **Dashed** (0.75 pt, 3 mm on / 2 mm off) | **Cut here** — nothing else | The 2-up exit slip cut line (with the scissors glyph) |
| Short-dash box (0.75 pt, 1.5 / 1 mm) | The unknown digit inside a stacked problem | Missing-digit box only |
| Square corners (radius 0) | The page's own structure | Cells, grid dividers, band strips, tables, number tabs, strand tab, header boxes |
| Slot radius **1.5 mm** | A place to WRITE | Answer boxes, digit strips, check boxes, number-bond boxes, answer squares |
| Container radius **3 mm** (1 mm on objects under 8 mm) | Something to read or think with, or a number object | Story box, vocabulary card, chant box, teacher boxes, number track, dot tile (1.2 mm) |

A 3 mm container is never an answer slot; a writing box is never above 1.5 mm. An unknown in a diagram is
a solid box marked `?` in its top-left corner, never dashed.

### 8.7 Frame: header, strand tab, title, footer, © line [WDS 8, PT-FRM, D9, D10]

```
|<--------------------------------- 186 mm --------------------------------->|
| Name ________________________  Date __________  Score ______ /12 +--------+|  row A 14 mm
|   (field rules 0.75 pt at y = 11 mm; labels 12 pt bold)          |Level 1 ||  strand tab
|                                                                  |Addition||  30 x 14 mm
|                                                                  |Practice||  1.5 pt
|                                                                  +--------+|
|                       I Can add 1–3 (facts to 10)                           |  gap 1 + title 8 mm, 18 pt bold
|==============================================================================|  gap 1.2 + 2.25 pt rule + gap 1
```
Total 26 mm. Parts:
- **Fields** (pupil pages with scored items): `Name` line (fills, 55–90 mm), `Date` line 26 mm, `Score`
  line 14 mm then `/N` at 15 pt, N = scored items on that page. Labels 12 pt bold; 5 mm between fields;
  rules 0.75 pt. No Score on a page with no scored item (anchor chart). On a key, the Name line holds the
  words `Answer Key` (15 pt bold, black).
- **Strand tab**: 30 × 14 mm, top-right, square corners, 1.5 pt outline, white, three centred lines at
  10 pt: `Level N` (bold) / strand ≤ 12 characters (bold) / page id (regular). Page ids:
  `Prerequisite`, `Number sense`, `Anchor chart`, `Lesson n`, `Practice n`, `Mixed n`, `Word problem`,
  `Exit slip`, `Answer Key`, `Teacher 1`…`Teacher 5`, `Reference`. Never a grade, CCSS code or skill id.
  **Owner default (2026-10-16, recommended; may change) — Q9:** the ids `Teacher n` and `Reference` are new.
- **Title**: `I Can <verb> <object> (<constraint>)` — "I Can" capitalised, sentence case, no full stop,
  ≤ 60 characters, centred, 18 pt bold, one line, **identical on every page of the pack** (teacher pages
  included). A title wider than the line wraps to 2 lines and takes 6 mm from the body; it never shrinks.
- **Lifted header (17 mm)**: when there are no fields (anchor chart, teacher pages) and the title is
  ≤ 118 mm wide, the title is centred in row A beside the tab: 14 mm row + 3 mm rule block.
- **Continuation header (12 mm)**: `Name` line (if fields) at left, a one-line tab (8 mm tall,
  "Level 1 · Addition · Teacher 3" at 9 pt) at right, then the 2.25 pt rule. No title, no Score. The
  instruction line repeats below it.
- **Footer** (6 mm band, 3 mm below the body, 7 pt): left `skill id · Grade n · CCSS · EE` (the only place
  grade and standards appear on a pupil page); centre `n/N` in bold; right `Form A · seed nnnn` (keys:
  `Answer Key · Form A · seed`; teacher pages: `Teacher · Form A · seed`).
- **© line** in the bottom margin, 1 mm under the footer, centred, 6.5 pt:
  `© <year> Cultivating the Digital. All rights reserved.` On every page.

### 8.8 Bands [WDS 9.1, BD-1…8, D11, D12]

A band is a full-width slice of the body with a label; cells live inside bands. Band strip: 8 mm tall,
label 15 pt bold, 3 mm from the left edge, ending in a colon, sentence case, then the instruction on the
same line at 15 pt regular. A band's top edge is a 2.25 pt rule; its other sides are 1.5 pt; adjacent
bands share the edge (overlap −1.5 pt). The first band under the header starts 1.5 mm below the header
rule with a 1.5 pt top.

Pupil band vocabulary (no other labels): `What's New:` `Vocabulary:` `Rule:` `Remember:` `Warm-up:`
`Steps:` `Model:` `Say:` `Guided Practice:` `Independent Practice:` `More Practice:` `Mixed Review:`
`Workspace:`, `Another example:` (anchor chart: the other cases, drawn all in black) and, new in V21 (**Owner default (2026-10-16, recommended; may change) — Q4**),
`Number sense: A` / `Number sense: B`. Never all capitals, never reversed out (white on black), never in a
tab, never rotated.

**`Say:` band**: closes the Model (anchor chart) and the word-problem and exit-slip pages. 13 mm tall; its
top edge is the shared 1.5 pt line (not a 2.25 pt rule); bold `Say:` at 15 pt then the oral frame at 15 pt
in curly quotes “…”, baseline 2.5 mm above the bottom edge. Blanks (≤ 2) are 0.75 pt lines B(n) wide; they
are said, not written, so the band is unscored. Frames come from Appendix C.3.

Teacher band labels (teacher pages only): `Lesson guidance:` `Lesson flow:` `Materials:` `Vocabulary:`
`What students need to know:` `What students do:` `How to teach:` `Common mistakes:` `Reference key:`
`Section n · Name (min)` `Prerequisite routing:` `Accepted equations:` `Answers at a glance:`.

### 8.9 Cells [WDS 7, CL-1…14, PG-10…15, D13]

- **CL-1** A body is one outer frame (1.5 pt) holding cells that share borders like a table (0.75 pt in
  the I Can look, 1.5 pt in the Daily look). No gutters, no floating or rounded cards, no shadows.
  Implementation: the grid's background is black and its `gap` is the border width (Appendix A §7).
- **CL-3** All cells of a section are the same size, computed once (grid rows and columns are `1fr`).
- **CL-4** The printed problem sits in the top half of its cell; spare height goes to the answer / work
  zone below it (PG-14), never above the problem.
- **CL-5** At least **40 %** of every item cell is free of ink (letters and slots count as ink; the open
  answer zone counts as free; a picture the pupil draws on counts as free).
- **CL-6** The slot sits in the same place in every cell of a section.
- **CL-7** One idea per cell: one problem, one visual, one answer type. No skill name, level or code.
- **CL-8** Padding 3 mm. Stacked-arithmetic cells: top pad 8 mm (room for the letter), sides 6 mm,
  bottom 4 mm.
- **CL-9** In a 1-column stacked layout the stack is centred in the leftmost 62 mm; the rest is open
  working space. (No V21 page uses a 1-column stack: the exit slip is a 2 × 2 grid, §9.7.)
- **CL-10** Quiet letter `a.` `b.` … at 10 pt, 1.5 mm from the top-left corners. Letters restart at `a.` on
  each page that is handed out alone (every pupil page in this pack). Model and Guided cells are
  unlabelled and not counted in the Score.
- **CL-30** Daily look (P1 only): a solid black number tab 6 × 6 mm flush in the cell's top-left corner,
  white 11 pt bold numeral (8.4 mm wide for 2 digits), numbering 1…N down the page.
- **No split cell** (PG-21): `break-inside: avoid` on every cell; a band is never split from its first
  row; a word problem with its routine panel, and a Model with its Steps, are keep-together units.
- **Empty bands** (RUBRIC H13, measured exactly as the site's `sheetCheck`): take the box around every
  content element of the cell — every leaf element (no child elements, or an SVG, or a slot) and every element
  with a border, the item letter excepted — and measure the gaps from that box to the cell's **border box**
  (the 3 mm padding counts as empty). The largest top-or-bottom gap ÷ cell height and the largest left-or-right
  gap ÷ cell width must each be **under 30 %** (cells under 40 px are skipped). A reserved answer row or slot
  is content, so the pupil's writing place is never "empty"; open space under it is. Text sitting loose in a
  container is not an element: wrap printed numbers and words in their own `<span>` so they count. The only
  exemption is a cell the pupil DRAWS or WORKS on (a `Workspace:` band, a picture to shade, partition, mark or
  plot, hops to draw, a work grid). Spare height means: add a row (OR-a), give the height to another band, or
  fix a short band's height to its content (content + 2 × 7 mm) — never leave it in the cells.
- **Trailing positions** (PG-15): item counts round to full rows; any remainder is one unruled blank
  area inside the frame, never empty lettered cells.

### 8.10 Answer slots [WDS 6, SL-1…12, D14]

The shape tells the pupil what kind of answer goes in it. Writing height **Hw = 10 mm** at L.

| Slot | Shape and size at L | Means |
|---|---|---|
| Line | 0.75 pt baseline, width B(n), clear height 10 mm above it | A number after `=` |
| Box | 0.75 pt, radius 1.5 mm, B(n) × 12 mm | A missing number inside an expression or sequence |
| Digit strip | One rounded outline, a 0.75 pt divider on every track boundary; answer strip 12 mm tall, regroup strip 8 mm | A row of digits under a column stack |
| Open answer row | 12 mm tall row under the sum rule (vertical facts) | A fact answer |
| Answer square | 24 × 24 mm, radius 1.5 mm | A count beside a picture |
| Circle | 0.75 pt, diameter 12 mm | A sign: + − × ÷ or < = > |
| Check box | 0.75 pt, 7 × 7 mm, radius 1.5 mm (5 mm on teacher pages) | A decision: check one box |
| Fraction | 1.5 pt bar B(2) wide, a 10 mm zone above and below | A fraction |
| Time | Two 20 × 12 mm boxes with a printed colon in a 5 mm gap | A time |
| Number + unit word | Line B(n), 2 mm gap, the unit word printed at 15 pt | A quantity in a word problem |
| Equation frame | `__ ○ __ = __`: lines for numbers, a circle for the sign | A number sentence |
| Words to circle | Printed words ≥ 8 mm apart, ring area ≥ 14 mm tall | A choice among printed words |

**B(n) = max(14, ceil(n × 0.75 × Hw + 2)) mm**, n = digits in the longest answer the section's band
allows (1 mm more per comma or point). At L: **B(1) = 14, B(2) = 17, B(3) = 25, B(4) = 33 mm** (with a
comma), B(5) = 41 mm. One width per section, so a slot's width never hints at its answer; "within 10"
gives n = 2 because 10 is a possible answer. Never draw a blank with underscore characters.

### 8.11 Owner rulings on page fill, problems per page and pictures (2026-10-16, word for word)

- **OR-a** "Every page is full. Spare height goes first to each card's work/answer space. Pictures may grow up to 1.25×
  their minimum, the same size in every card of the section. Text and digit sizes are fixed by the lesson size and never
  change from page to page. If space is still left, add a problem (up to the page's ceiling) — never enlarge the
  content."
- **OR-b (revised 2026-10-16)** "The We Do and You Do can have as many problems as make sense and will be clear
  according to our design specifications." *(This replaces the earlier ruling "We Do and You Do pages hold 4–6
  problems: 6 by default (2 × 3), 4 for long procedures; word problems 1–2 per page; full-width visuals 3–5".)*
  The count therefore comes from the capacity rules of §8.12.1: the ceiling for the page role at size L, every
  item cell ≥ 40 % free, no page under 50 % used, no lone item, no empty band ≥ 30 %, content never shrinks.
  Fill to the clearest count those rules allow, never past a ceiling.
- **OR-c** "A picture is the same size in every card of a page, up to 1.25× its minimum. When the pupil works ON the
  picture (shade, partition, mark, plot, draw), the picture is the answer zone and takes the spare height."

How V21 applies them [AUD §3a builder notes]:
- OR-a's "add a problem" comes after the work zones are filled and stops at the role's ceiling (§8.12.1).
- **The anchor chart is the one exception to "never enlarge":** it is a wall poster, so its step drawings
  enlarge to fill the page, never below 0.8× of the size they would have elsewhere (other examples ≥ 0.85×)
  [LR-15]. In practice: chart digits 34 pt, other examples 30 pt.
- OR-b covers a We Do or You Do *page*. The lesson sheet's `Guided Practice:` band beside `Steps:` stays
  2–3 cells [LR-4], sized to their content; a separate Guided page holds 3–6.
- One-symbol answers (vertical facts, "27 → __", fact rows) follow the same capacity rules: up to **16 per
  page** at L (site precedent: 3 × 4 = 12 vertical facts; 2 × 5 / 2 × 8 grids for one-line answers). Under the
  revised OR-b this is no longer a separate owner default.

### 8.12 Decision rules

#### 8.12.1 Problems per page [OR-b, WDS 12.1, D18]

Ceilings at size L (WDS 12.1, one section, A4 full header). A ceiling is never exceeded; the count is the
largest one that passes the capacity test below.

| Page role / item | Ceiling at L | Usual grid |
|---|---|---|
| Prerequisite Check | 3–4 questions (one per Pre-requisite atom), up to 8 items | one band per question |
| Number sense | 5 + 5 | Daily-look rows |
| Anchor chart | 1 worked example in 2–5 panels + 1–3 other examples | rows or 2 × 3 panels |
| Lesson sheet Guided band | 2–3 (beside `Steps:`) | 1 row |
| Lesson sheet Independent band | whole rows of 3 in the remaining height | 1–2 rows |
| Guided page | 3–6 | 2 × 2 – 2 × 3 |
| Independent / Practice, standard | 6 | 2 × 3 |
| Independent / Practice, long procedure (3-digit regrouping, long × or ÷) | 4 | 2 × 2 |
| Independent / Practice, one-symbol answers | 16 | 3 × 4 vertical facts; 2 × 5, 2 × 8 one-line |
| Wide visuals (number lines, coin rows, base-10 over 100) | 3 | full-width rows |
| Word problem v1 (scaffolded) / v2 (faded) | 1 / 2 | — |
| Exit slip | 4 per half (one per case) | 2 × 2 |

```
function itemsPerPage(page):
  minCellH = template minimum height (content + pads + one Hw under a written answer)   # 8.9
  minCellW = max(item width, slot width) + pads
  cols = floor(186 / minCellW)            clamp to the usual grid's columns
  rows = floor(gridH / minCellH)
  n = min(cols * rows, ceiling(page.role, item))
  while n > 1:
      lay out n items in equal cells (cells take the whole grid height)
      if every cell >= 40 % free  and  no empty band >= 30 %  and  page >= 50 % used: return n
      if the cells are too EMPTY (band >= 30 %): add a row of items if the ceiling allows, else
         give the height to another band (Guided, Workspace, Vocabulary), else shrink the band to content
      if the cells are too FULL (< 40 % free): n -= one row
  never return 1 unless the role's ceiling is 1 (word problem v1)
```

#### 8.12.2 Page fill order [OR-a]

```
1. Fix sizes: type, digits, Hw, B(n), picture minimums (size L). These never change.
2. Choose the grid from 8.12.1 (columns x rows); cells are equal (1fr x 1fr) and fill the body exactly.
3. Spare height in every cell goes to its answer / work zone only while every band stays under 30 % (§8.9,
   measured from the border box; no cap for a draw / work cell). More spare than that: step 5.
4. Pictures may grow to <= 1.25 x their minimum, the same size in every cell of the section (8.12.3).
5. Still spare and the page below its ceiling? add a row of problems. Recompute from step 2.
6. Banded pages (lesson sheet, word problem) give the spare height to the bands in this order:
   Independent / Guided cells, then Workspace, then Vocabulary.
7. Check: page >= 50 % used; no cell band >= 30 % empty; every item cell >= 40 % free (Appendix E).
   If a check fails, change the grid or the section heights, never the type size.
```

#### 8.12.3 Pictures and the spare height [OR-c, RP-3]

| If | Then |
|---|---|
| The pupil only reads the picture (count, read the time, compare) | Draw it at its L minimum (Appendix D), up to 1.25×, same size in every cell of the section; the spare height goes to the answer zone |
| The pupil works ON the picture (shade, partition, mark, plot, draw hops or hands, draw counters) | The picture IS the answer zone: it grows to fill its cell (same size in every cell), and it counts as free space |
| It is the anchor chart | Step drawings enlarge to fill the poster, ≥ 0.8× (other examples ≥ 0.85×) |
| The picture's size could hint at the answer (bar lengths, number of boxes) | Draw it schematic and constant across the section (bars equal length; same track counts) |
| The picture will not fit at its minimum | Drop a column or a row; never shrink it |
| One item holds two pictures side by side (for example two base-ten numbers to compare) | Width test: picture 1 + picture 2 + 6 mm gap + 2 × 3 mm pads (+ the slot if it sits beside them). If that is more than the column width (93 mm in 2 columns, 62 mm in 3), the item is a **wide visual**: full-width rows, 3 per page at L. Example: two base-ten pictures of 50.5 mm → 50.5 + 50.5 + 6 + 6 = 113 mm > 93 mm → full-width rows. |

#### 8.12.4 Pagination and rebalancing [PG-20…24]

```
if content does not fit:
   1. move items (whole rows) to the next page of the same section;
   2. reduce the column count;
   3. split the section into a second page of the same role.
   Never reduce type, Hw, B(n) or a picture below its minimum.
a cell is never split; a band is never separated from its first row of cells;
the instruction line repeats, word for word, at the top of every page on which its section continues;
continuation pages use the 12 mm header;
if the last page would hold fewer than 1/3 of a full page's rows, rebalance the rows evenly over the pages;
a page under 50 % used is merged into the page before or filled with more items (up to the ceiling).
```

#### 8.12.5 Scaffold fade [WDS 13.1, PED 4, D15]

**★ has one meaning: the fade level** (★ Guided, ★★ Independent, ★★★ edge-case row). It never marks a
prerequisite; a prerequisite atom is tagged with the word `Pre-requisite`.

| Scaffold | Class | Anchor chart (Model) | Guided (★) | Independent (★★) | Practice last row (★★★) / Exit / Test |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Trace of the step's mark (ring round the big number, written answer) | Hint | black, newest mark grey | cell 1 only, grey (traced) | none | none |
| Cue picture (dot tile, ten frame beside the number) | Hint | black (newest grey) | grey, every Guided cell | none (or first cell only, grey) | none |
| `Steps:` list with icons | Hint | the chart's step panels | beside the Guided cells | step strip at the top of Practice pages | none on the exit slip |
| Captions, word bank, hop track | Hint | black | grey | first cell only | none |
| Digit grid, regroup strip, T O heads, frames, unit word, number-bond boxes, equation frame | **Structural** | stays | stays | stays | stays |

The instruction is the same in every fade band of a lesson; fading changes scaffolds, never wording.

#### 8.12.6 Slot choice [SL-3, SL-7]

```
answer after '='                         -> line, width B(n)
blank inside an expression or sequence   -> box (and every blank in that section becomes a box)
a row of digits under a column stack     -> digit strip
vertical fact answer                     -> open answer row (12 mm) under the sum rule
a count beside a picture (K-1)           -> answer square 24 mm
a sign                                   -> circle 12 mm
a decision                               -> check boxes (7 mm), one per printed option
a word from a bank                       -> words to circle (never a free line)
a word-problem quantity                  -> line + printed unit word
a production item on paper               -> stays production on screen and on the key (never multiple choice)
```

#### 8.12.7 Answer keys [AK-1…4, INK-31, D6]

- Every pupil page that has a slot gets a **facsimile key**: the same page, same layout, same slots, with
  every answer written in its own slot in **#C2410C Andika 700 at 28 pt** (lines: on the line; boxes:
  centred; circles and choices: a 1.5 pt key-coloured ring; matching: key-coloured lines between the
  dots; drawings: the marks the pupil would add, in key colour).
- Only answer marks take the key colour. Every printed number, word and line stays black.
- The key's strand tab line 3 reads `Answer Key`; the Name line holds the words `Answer Key`; the footer
  right reads `Answer Key · Form A · seed …`.
- The Prerequisite Check key also prints, right-aligned in each section strip at 11 pt black,
  `If missed → Lesson <id> <title>`.
- The anchor chart also gets a key page: the same chart with the tab `Answer Key` (it has no slots, so it is
  otherwise identical). Every pupil page has exactly one key.
- Keys print after the teacher pages, in pupil page order, then the T6 routing page.

#### 8.12.8 Prerequisite routing [LR-17]

```
P0 holds 3-4 questions, most basic first; each comes from ONE earlier lesson (its skill and options).
for each pupil:
   misses = questions answered wrong
   if misses == 0:            continue the lesson
   if misses == 1:            teach that prerequisite lesson first (small group), then return
   if misses >= 2:            start with the EARLIEST missed prerequisite, then the next, then return
a prerequisite lesson that does not exist yet is listed on T6 as "Lesson to write: <topic>".
```

#### 8.12.9 Which pages exist, and their order [D17, LV r1-r4]

Teacher pack order: **T1 · T2 · T2b (once per unit) · T3 · T4 · T5 · pupil packet P0–P6 (one master copy)
· T6 (facsimile keys in pupil order, then the routing page)**. The pupil packet order is: **P0 Prerequisite
Check · P1 Number sense (optional, default on) · P2 Anchor chart · P3 Lesson sheet · P4 Practice 1…n (+
optional Mixed) · P5 Word problem · P6 Exit slip (2-up)**.

**Owner default (2026-10-16, recommended; may change) — Q1:** when the teacher supplies an existing
MathQuest lesson packet for the skill (its pupil pages already passed review), use those pupil pages as
P0/P2/P3/P4 and generate only the teacher pages, P1, P5 and P6 around them. Flip: always build every
P-page from Part 9.

### 8.13 Owner defaults in force (2026-10-16, recommended; may change)

| Q | Question | V21 builds with | Where it applies | To flip |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | Use the site's pupil packet when one exists? | Yes | §8.12.9 | Always build P-pages |
| Q2 | Paper | A4 default, Letter on request | §8.1 | Letter default |
| Q3 | Printed routed Prerequisite Check replaces warm-up + MWB check; retrieval on MWB | Yes | Budget, §9.1, T3 | Restore V20 1a/1b/2 |
| Q4 | Number sense: separate optional Daily-look page (bands A / B) | Yes, default on | §9.2, bank intro | Oral only, no P1 |
| Q5 | Word problem: RCDWS check-box panel | Yes | §9.6 | Schema diagram instead |
| Q6 | Third-party problems: rewrite in our words | Yes | Part 18 STEP 4 | Verbatim without our © line |
