Chunked Flow.** Skip it only for very simple single-step routines (fewer than 3 steps total).

**Delivery:**

1.  DO: Point to the I Do example on the board (or write the first line of it without solving).
    SAY: *"You just showed me [Atom 1]. You also showed me [Atom 2]. Now watch what happens
    when I put those two things together — that's exactly what this lesson is about."*

    > Note: Keep this under 60 seconds. It is a signpost, not a pre-lesson. The teacher
    > names the atoms students already know and signals that the I Do is the moment they are
    > combined. Do not introduce new content here.

Numbered circle: ②b

---

#### Section 3 — Instruction ("I Do") — Silent Teacher (12 Minutes)

> **V21 changes to this section.** Now **Section 5 · I Do (12 min)**. Keep Steps 0–5 exactly. Step 1 "display the visual model alongside" = the **anchor chart** is the board copy (Board = Paper): each step is a panel in the skill's own drawing, newest marks grey. "CRA stage in a green callout" becomes a plain `CRA stage: Pictures` line. Labelling a wrong example on the board stays board work: write the word Wrong beside it; it is never printed on pupil pages (Find the mistake is paused).


> **SECTION LESSON TIP PLACEMENT:** Render a `Tip:` box at the top of this section.
> Label: Tip: [memorable phrase].
> Draw from the library: **Silent Demo, Expert Narration, Economy of Language, Dual Coding,
> 2.13 Non-Example Contrast, CLT Transient Information Effect, CLT Modality Effect,
> Variation Theory, Self-Explanation, Schema Connection.**
> Address: the key teaching move for this specific content, orientation variation reminder
> for spatial topics, or which step is the Critical Atom to highlight.
> (e.g.  "Silence First, Then Name It — model all steps before speaking")
>
> Place a Watch: box after Step 4 (Wrong Example) if a specific misconception warrants it.
> Place a Research: box before Step 1 if a non-obvious finding directly governs delivery.

**What this section is:** The teacher works through the new skill in front of
the class. Steps 0–5 are non-negotiable — do not swap or skip any of them.

**Delivery steps:**

0.  **SCHEMA CONNECTION HOOK (30 seconds — spoken before anything is written)**

    DO: Stand at the board. Nothing written yet.
    SAY: *"Last [lesson / week / unit] we learned [prior skill — name it specifically].
    Today we are adding [new skill]. Once you can do this, you will be able to
    [next skill this unlocks — one sentence]."*

    > Note: This is a mathematical signpost, not motivation. Two sentences naming
    > where this lesson fits in the topic map. Specific is essential — "Last week we
    > partitioned 3-digit numbers" not "we've been doing place value." Vague hooks
    > do not prime schema. Keep to 30 seconds.

1.  **SILENT DEMONSTRATION WITH VISUAL MODEL**

    DO: Work through Example 1 completely — every line of working — without speaking.
    Students watch. As you work, display the visual model (bar model / number line /
    area model / diagram) alongside the written example — side by side or directly
    below, never on a separate page.
    DO: Show CRA stage in a green callout box on the teacher page.
    If Representational or Abstract stage:
    SAY: “Remember when we used [physical object]? This drawing shows the same idea — now we use numbers.”

    > Note: The silence ensures students attend to what is written, not compete
    > between reading and listening. Write EVERY step — including steps that seem
    > obvious. The visual model is not decoration — for ELL students it is the primary
    > non-linguistic entry point. It must appear now, alongside, never separately.

2.  **NARRATION — EXPERT THINK-ALOUD**

    DO: Put the marker down. Point back to the completed example.
    SAY: “Now let me explain what I did and why.”
    [Narration — lesson-specific, 3–5 short sentences. Explain WHY each step
    happens, not just WHAT. Every key phrase written permanently on the board —
    do not rely on spoken words alone.]
    SAY: “Notice [the Critical Atom step — named plainly].”

    > Note: The narration explains the WHY behind work already on the board —
    > it does not repeat what is written (Redundancy Effect). Write every verbal
    > anchor as you say it — spoken words disappear immediately (Transient Information
    > Effect). This is especially critical for ELL students processing both the
    > mathematics and the English at the same time.

3.  **EXAMPLE 2 WITH VISUAL MODEL**

    DO: Work through Example 2 silently (different numbers, same procedure).
    Display its visual model alongside. Narrate briefly — WHY not WHAT.

    > Note — ORIENTATION/PRESENTATION VARIATION (mandatory for spatial, graphical,
    > or geometric topics): Example 2 must change the visual presentation or orientation,
    > not just the numbers. Fraction strips shown only horizontally, right angles only
    > in standard position, bar graphs always vertical — these lock students into
    > recognising the look rather than the concept.
    > Geometric shapes: rotate or flip. Graphs: swap axes or change scale.
    > Measurement: change units or orientation.
    > Purely numerical topics (addition, place value): different numbers are enough.

4.  **WRONG EXAMPLE**

    DO: Write the wrong example — the most common mistake from T2 Section 4 — on
    the board. Label it clearly: the word Wrong.
    SAY: “Watch — here is what goes wrong when [plain description of the mistake].”
    SAY: “See what happens? [Point to the exact error.] This mistake is called [plain name].”
    ASK: “What went wrong? Tell your partner. 15 seconds.”
    DO: Cold call one student.

    > Note: Students who see the error before they practise are measurably
    > less likely to make it. Do not skip or rush this step.

5.  **SELF-EXPLANATION PAUSE + TRANSITION**

    DO: Point to the Critical Atom step on the board.
    SAY: *"Look at this step. Quietly — why do we do this step here?
    Write one reason. Mini whiteboards. 60 seconds. No talking."*
    DO: Circulate and read what students write. Do not comment aloud yet.
    If most students write a correct reason → proceed.
    If most are blank or wrong → give the reason in one plain sentence:
    SAY: “The reason is [explanation].”
    SAY: “Keep that reason in mind. Let's practise together.”
    → Move to Section 4.

    > Note: The 60-second silence gives every student — including ELL students
    > who need longer to form a written response in English — time to engage before
    > the class moves on. Do not shorten it.

Numbered circle: ③

---

#### Section 4 — Guided Practice ("We Do" — 8 Minutes)

> **V21 changes to this section.** Now **Section 6 · We Do (8 min)**. The pairing, think-aloud and partner talk stay. The "purple box" sentence frame becomes `Say:` lines on the teacher page; the expected answer is in the key colour. Pupil page: the `Guided Practice:` band beside `Steps:` (2–3 cells, cell 1 with step 1 in grey).


> **SECTION TIP PLACEMENT:** Render a `.section-tip` box at the top of this section.
> Draw from the library: **Example-Problem Pairs, Cold Call, No Opt-Out, Wait Time,
> Turn and Talk, Right is Right, Say It Again Better, Sentence Starters, Student
> 2.3 Think-Aloud, Rosenshine P7 High Success Rate, 4.11 Elaborative Interrogation.**
> Address: the most common We Do mistake AND the sentence frame that unlocks ELL responses.
> (e.g.  "They'll Add — Show the Gap" /  "Say 'difference', not 'answer'")

**What this section is:** Teacher and students work through problems together.
Students answer on mini whiteboards. Teacher circulates, looks at boards, and
gives feedback before students try independently.

**Format:** 2–3 tasks, getting slightly harder (★ first, then ★★).

**Partner pairing — assign before the lesson starts:**
Pair ELL students with a peer who is slightly more English-proficient — not the
strongest English speaker in the class. Too large a gap causes the ELL student to
listen rather than produce language, which removes the schema-building benefit of
talk. Write pairs on the board at the lesson start so the assignment is visible,
consistent, and removes any social negotiation mid-lesson.

**Delivery steps:**

1.  DO: Direct students to the We Do section on their worksheet (P3).
    SAY: “We're going to try this together. Mini whiteboards ready.”
    SAY: “[Task 1 — read aloud clearly].”
    DO: Give students 1–2 minutes to attempt Task 1.
    SAY: “Show me in 3, 2, 1.”

    > Note: If most boards are correct → step 2. If many show the same
    > wrong answer → address that specific mistake before continuing (60 seconds).

1b. **STUDENT THINK-ALOUD (Task 2 only — before boards go up):**
    DO: Read Task 2 aloud. Before students begin:
    SAY: *"This time, whisper your steps as you go. Say each step out loud to yourself
    as you write it."*
    DO: Give 1–2 minutes. Walk around and listen — you are not looking at boards yet,
    you are listening for which steps students narrate correctly and which they skip or name wrongly.
    SAY: “Show me in 3, 2, 1.”

    > Note: The think-aloud is not partner talk — students whisper to themselves, not
    > to each other. You are listening for silent gaps (steps students cannot name) and
    > wrong vocabulary (steps students mislabel). Both tell you more than a mini-whiteboard
    > answer alone. ELL students particularly benefit because academic language is being
    > produced — not just a number — while the physical action grounds its meaning.
    > Only use the think-aloud on Task 2 (not Task 1 or Task 3) to keep the We Do moving.

2.  DO: Put the sentence frame on the board (as a `Say:` line on the teacher page).
    Show the expected answer in the key colour (#C2410C, bold) directly below the
    blank(s) on the teacher delivery page — so the teacher sees the target response
    without students needing to see it on their copy.
    Format on teacher page:
    `"I will __________ because I am finding the __________."`
    `Expected: "I will subtract because I am finding the difference."`  *(key colour)*
    SAY: “Talk to your partner: [specific question about this task]. Partner A starts. 30 seconds. Go.”
    DO: After 30 seconds:
    ASK: “Partner B — what did your partner say?”
    DO: Cold call one pair. Listen for correct reasoning, not just the answer.

    > Note: Always give students three things before partner talk: WHAT to
    > discuss, WHO goes first, HOW LONG. Without all three, talk drifts.
    > The sentence frame lets English language learners start without worrying
    > about vocabulary. The key-colour expected answer on the teacher page means the
    > teacher knows what a complete, correct response sounds like before calling on anyone.

3.  DO: Repeat steps 1–2 for Task 2 (★★).

4.  DO: Before moving to the Check Question, scan the room.
    ASK yourself: Are most students getting ★★ correct?
    If yes → move to Section 5.
    If no → do one more task at ★★ before moving on.

    > Note: Do not rush to independent practice. One extra task here saves
    > a lot of confusion in Section 6.

Numbered circle: ④

---

#### Section 5 — Check Question (3 Minutes)

> **V21 changes to this section.** Now **Section 7 · Check Question (3 min)**, board and mini whiteboards only, never printed. Correct / wrong option colours become a check mark beside the right option (key colour) and the mistake name in bold beside each wrong one.


> **SECTION TIP PLACEMENT:** Render a `.section-tip` box at the top of this section.
> Draw from the library: **1.14 Hinge Questions, 1.4 Replace Self-Report, 1.15 Responsive Teaching,
> 4.10 Excavate Error, 1.12 Culture of Error.**
> Address: what each wrong answer reveals and which mistake to address first.
> (e.g.  "Wrong Answer A = They Totalled, Not Compared")

**What this section is:** One multiple-choice question that tells the teacher
whether to move on, fix a specific mistake, or reteach. Every wrong answer
corresponds to a specific mistake from T2. This is the teacher's decision point.

**Format:** 3 answer choices. One correct (a check mark and the key colour on the teacher page). Two wrong —
each matching a named mistake from the Common Mistakes table on T2 (shown in
plain English, bold black, on the teacher page).

**Delivery steps:**

1.  DO: Display or read the Check Question aloud.
    SAY: “One question. Think on your own first. 30 seconds.”
    DO: Wait. Do not give hints.

2.  SAY: “Show me in 3, 2, 1.”
    DO: Scan all boards before anyone flips or changes their answer.
    DO: Count roughly — how many show A? B? C?

3.  DO: Use the data loop below. Pick the row that matches what you saw.

---

**DATA LOOP — look at the boards and pick one:**

(hollow circle) Fewer than half: **Fewer than half got it right:**
    DO: Do not move on. Go back to the board.
    SAY: *"[Verbatim reteach script — lesson-specific, completely different
    example from the I Do.]"*
    DO: One fresh mini whiteboard question. Scan boards again.

(half circle) About half: **About half got it right:**
    SAY: “A lot of people chose [wrong answer]. Here's why that's a tricky one.”
    DO: Show and correct the specific mistake (named in plain English).
    SAY: “Try this one.” [One more quick mini whiteboard task.]

(solid circle) Most: **Most got it right:**
    SAY: “Good. Open your worksheet to the practice section.”
    → Move to Section 6.

---

> Note: The wrong answers are not random — each one matches a specific
> mistake that real students make. The teacher page shows what each wrong
> answer means in plain English and exactly what to say about it.

Numbered circle: ⑤

---

#### Section 6 — Independent Practice ("You Do" — 20 Minutes)

> **V21 changes to this section.** Now **Section 8 · You Do (15 min)** (the V20 "20 Minutes" header is corrected to 15). "Grades 3–5 target 8–10" becomes the Practice counts of §8.12.1 (6 per page, 4 for long procedures, one-symbol answers up to 12–16). ★ / ★★ / ★★★ are the fade levels of Part 7. Circulation, peer-tutoring and early-finisher scripts stay; early-finisher Option C ("Write one sentence that explains WHY") becomes an oral frame.


> **SECTION TIP PLACEMENT:** Render a `.section-tip` box at the top of this section.
> Draw from the library: **1.13 Circulate, 1.7 Active Observation, 3.12 Corrective Feedback Loop,
> Hattie Process-Level Feedback, 4.10 Excavate Error, 1.11 Own and Track, 6.6 Positive Framing,
> 7.9 Strategic Pairing, CLT Expertise Reversal, 1.8 Affirmative Checking, 6.8 Warm/Strict.**
> Address: circulation priority and the process question for the most common mistake.
> (e.g.  "Visit ELL/SPED first — ask 'What step is this?' not 'What's the answer?'")

**What this section is:** Students work through problems on their own.
The teacher circulates, visits struggling students first, and gives targeted
feedback without giving away answers.

**Problem set — grade band determines the target count (see Grade Band spec):**
- **PK–2:** Minimum 3 problems, target 5–6 (not counting the word problem).
- **Grades 3–5:** Practice pages hold the count of §8.12.1 (6 a page; 4 for long procedures; up to 16 one-symbol answers), 1–2 pages, plus the lesson-sheet Independent rows (not counting the word problem).
Aim closer to the target than the minimum. If the source PDF contains more problems
than the target, use all of them — there is no upper limit from a source worksheet.
All problems are paper-based — on the generated student worksheet or on a
referenced printed sheet. Never delivered verbally or on mini whiteboards.

**Fade structure (§8.12.5):**
- ★ (Guided, on the lesson sheet): cell 1 has step 1 in grey; hint scaffolds grey in every Guided cell.
- ★★ (Independent rows and Practice rows): structure only — digit grid, regroup strip, frames, unit word.
- ★★★ (the last Practice row): the lesson's edge cases in the SAME cell format. Cross-topic or reversed items
  only if the lesson taught them.

> Note: The two ★ scaffolds work differently and reinforce each other. The completion
> problem removes the production demand for steps the student cannot yet carry out reliably,
> keeping working memory free for the step being practised. The text hint activates the
> relevant rule or strategy in long-term memory before the student begins. Together they
> provide both structural and cognitive support — the strongest combination for ELL and
> SPED students at the acquisition phase.

**Delivery steps:**

1.  DO: Direct students to the practice section on their worksheet.
    SAY: “[Lesson-specific instruction to begin — read aloud.]”
    DO: Immediately walk to your 4 most-likely-to-struggle students
    (English language learners and students with special education support first).
    Visit them within the first 60 seconds.

    > Note: The first 60 seconds of independent practice are the most
    > important. Students who are confused will spend 20 minutes staring at
    > the page if you don't catch them early.

2.  DO: While circulating, look for the specific mistakes listed in the `Watch:`
    box on this page [mistake names in plain English and what they look like].
    When you spot a mistake, point to the specific step — do not mark it.
    ASK: “[Process question for most common mistake — e.g. 'Which step do you do first? Point to it.']”
    ASK: “[Process question for second mistake — lesson-specific]”

    > Note: Do not give the answer. Ask a question that points to the step
    > where things went wrong. If the student still can't find the error after
    > a second question, draw a simpler version of the problem and try again.

3.  DO: When a student gets ★ correct three times in a row:
    SAY: “Show me this one without the hints.” [Point to a ★★ problem.]

    DO: When a student is stuck on ★ after two tries:
    Sit down with them. Work through one example step by step — they do each step
    after you model it. Do not move them to ★★ until they complete ★ alone.

4.  DO: When a student finishes ★★ quickly and correctly:
    SAY: “Try this one — no hints.” [Point to the ★★★ problem.]

    > Note: Moving students up or down costs zero extra time — it just
    > changes what you say when you visit them. Every student should be working
    > at the edge of their ability, not waiting or drowning.

5.  **PEER TUTORING TRIGGER (use once per lesson, teacher-assigned only):**
    DO: When a student has completed ★★ correctly three or more times AND you
    have a student still working on ★ who is no longer confused — just slow —
    pair them briefly:
    SAY to the tutor: *"Show [Name] what you did at the specific step.
    Just that one step. Don't give the answer — show the move."*
    SAY to the tutee: “Watch, then try it yourself.”
    DO: Check back within 2 minutes. Both students remain accountable — the tutee
    must complete the problem themselves. The tutor then returns to their own ★★★ work.

    > Note: Peer tutoring works because explaining forces the tutor to
    > reconstruct and strengthen their own schema (Slavin, 1996). It is NOT
    > free choice pairing and NOT group work — the teacher assigns it, scripts it
    > to one specific step, and both students are checked on return. Never pair a
    > student who is still confused (★ after two wrong attempts) — sit with that
    > student yourself. Peer tutoring is for the student who is secure but slow,
    > not the student who is lost.

6.  **EARLY FINISHER — SCHEMA EXTENSION (for students who complete ★★★):**
    DO: When a student finishes all ★★★ problems, approach them quietly.
    Choose ONE of the following extensions based on what the lesson's content allows:

    **Option A — Create a variation:**
    SAY: *"Turn your paper over. Write a new problem just like Question [n] — but
    change one number to make it harder. Then solve it and check your answer."*

    **Option B — Test the method:**
    SAY: *"Look at Question [n]. Change one number in the problem.
    Does the same method still work? Show me."*

    **Option C — Explain the rule:**
    SAY: *"Write one sentence that explains WHY the method works for this type of problem.
    Someone who has never seen this before should be able to understand it."*

    > Note: These are Schema Extension moves — not new content, not busywork.
    > Option A tests whether students can generate problems (the highest-level
    > production task). Option B tests the boundary of the method (where does it
    > break down?). Option C requires explicit articulation of the concept — the
    > hardest cognitive demand and the most revealing for assessment.
    > Use Option C sparingly for ELL students — it requires significant English
    > production. Options A and B are accessible regardless of language level.
    > Never send early finishers to a new topic. Deepen, don't accelerate.

Numbered circle: ⑥

---

#### Section 6b — Word Problem (7–8 minutes, within You Do time)

> **V21 changes to this section.** Now **Section 9 · Word problem (in You Do)**, pupil page P5 (§9.6). The italic bordered box becomes the story box (no italic); the 2 × 2 emoji grid is removed. Delivery steps 1–5 stay.


**What this section is:** Every student works through one word problem using
the Read, Circle, Draw, Write, Solve steps. This is the ★★ level — one version
only. The sentence frame and word bank do the scaffolding.

**Student page format (P5):**
- Problem statement in the story box (§9.6), no italic
- The pupil page is P5 (§9.6): story box with its answer line, the six-line RCDWS check-box panel, the
  `Workspace:` band, the equation frame and the `Say:` band. No emoji, no word-bank chips on v1 beyond a
  bank of ≤ 4 words on the Guided form, no sentence writing. Answers are on the key and the teacher page only.

**Delivery steps:**

1.  DO: Direct students to the word problem page (P5).
    SAY: “Read the problem to yourself first.”
    DO: Read the problem aloud once at normal speed.
    SAY: “Now circle the numbers. Underline the question. 30 seconds.”

    > Note: Reading aloud once before students start is one of the most
    > effective supports for English language learners. Hearing the words at
    > normal speed helps them understand the story before wrestling with the
    > vocabulary. Do this every time.

2.  DO: Point to the Draw It section on the student page.
    SAY: “Draw a picture of what's happening. It doesn't have to be perfect — just show the numbers. One minute.”

3.  SAY: “Write your number sentence in the Write It box. Solve it. Then fill in the sentence at the bottom using the word bank.”
    DO: Circulate. Look at drawings first — a student who can't draw the problem usually can't solve it either.

4.  DO: After most students have finished, place one student's work on the
    visualiser/board. Ask permission first. Show incorrect work anonymously.
    ASK: “What did they draw? Does the drawing match the problem?”
    ASK: “What number sentence did they write? Does it make sense?”

5.  DO: Deliver the Consolidation Script (written verbatim on teacher page):
    [Script — lesson-specific. Must include: one "Why did we…" question,
    one expected student response, one self-check question.]

Numbered circle: ⑥b

---

#### Section 7 — Plenary — Exit Ticket + Consolidation (7 Minutes)

> **V21 changes to this section.** Now **Section 10 · Plenary (7 min)**. The exit question on mini whiteboards stays; the written reflection page is replaced by the 2-up exit slip P6 (Owner default Q8). The thumbs check stays oral.


> **SECTION TIP PLACEMENT:** Render a `.section-tip` box at the top of this section.
> Draw from the library: **Metacognition, Self-Efficacy, Show Call, Culture of Error,
> 6.7 Precise Praise, 1.3 Exit Ticket, 4.11 Elaborative Interrogation, 5.9 Confidence Scoring.**
> Address: what to listen for in the reflection and who to follow up with tomorrow.
> Skip if no specific watchout exists for this lesson's plenary.
> (e.g.  "Flat Hands = Placeholder Confusion — note names for tomorrow's opener")

**What this section is:** A quick check that students can do the skill
independently, followed by a written reflection to consolidate the learning.

**Delivery steps:**

1.  DO: Ask students to put their worksheets down.
    SAY: “One last question. Mini whiteboards.”
    SAY: “[Exit ticket question — lesson-specific, ★★ level].”
    DO: Give 45 seconds.
    SAY: “Show me in 3, 2, 1.”

2.  DO: Choose two boards to show on the visualiser/board:
    - One correct board (ask permission, or show anonymously)
    - One board with the most common mistake (always anonymous)
    ASK: “What's the same about these two? What's different?”
    ASK: “What went wrong on this one?” [Point to the wrong one.]

    > Note: Showing both correct and incorrect side by side is more powerful
    > than only praising the right answer. Students learn from the contrast.
    > Always show the mistake anonymously — this matters for trust.

3.  DO: Direct students to the Reflection section on student sheet P6.
    SAY: “Fill in the reflection at the bottom. Use the word bank. Two minutes.”

4.  DO: Deliver the Reflection Consolidation Script (written verbatim on teacher page).
    [Lesson-specific script — must end with the goal check below.]
    ASK: “Can you now [I Can statement]? Thumbs up if yes. Flat hand if you need one more day. Both are fine.”
    DO: Look at the room. Note flat hands — check in with those students first tomorrow.

    > Note: The thumbs check is not a grade. Tell students there is no wrong
    > answer and that flat hand means "I need one more day." This normalises
    > honest self-assessment and gives you useful information for tomorrow.

Numbered circle: ⑦

---

### PART 4: Answer Key (Teacher Page T6) + Inline Answers in Delivery Script

**Inline answers in the delivery script (T3–T5) — MANDATORY:**

Expected answers appear directly in the script, in the key colour **#C2410C, Andika 700** (the colour of
the key the teacher marks with). One treatment for every kind of answer: numbers, equations, the filled
words of an oral frame. No underlining, no italics, no second colour.

**Placement by section:**
- **Prerequisite Check (T3, Section 1):** answers in a `Note:` line + the routing pointer to T6.
- **Number sense (T3, Section 2):** band A and band B answers in one `Note:` line each.
- **Spaced retrieval (T3, Section 3):** the answer after each `Ask:`.
- **I Do / We Do (T4):** the answer at the end of the step that produces it; the filled oral frame after
  `Say:`.
- **Check Question (T5, Section 7):** the right option in key colour with a check mark; each wrong option
  followed by its mistake name in bold black (no green / pink).
- **You Do (T5, Section 8):** a pointer to T6 ("Answers at a glance").
- **Word problem (T5, Section 9):** every accepted equation and the answer with its unit.
- **Exit slip (T5, Section 10):** the answer.

**T6 Answer Key — facsimiles, not lists [AK-1…4, AUD C-08]:**
- One facsimile key per pupil page with slots, in packet order: the same page with every answer in its
  own slot in #C2410C Andika 700 at 28 pt (§8.12.7). Tab line 3 `Answer Key`; `Answer Key` on the Name
  line; footer right `Answer Key · Form A · seed`.
- Then the routing page: `Prerequisite routing:` table (Question · Prerequisite · If missed → Lesson),
  `Accepted equations:` for the word problem, `Answers at a glance:` (one row per pupil page), and the
  note that the anchor chart needs no key.
- Show working only where a pupil writes working (regroup strips, the drawing on P5).
- No banner, no "TEACHER USE ONLY" line, no list-only key.

---
### PART 5: Embedded Teaching and Feedback Boxes

> **V21 changes to this section.** Keep the four box types and their decision rules. Render them as T-BOX-1…4 (§10.5): black only, line-art icons, no emoji; `Watch:` has the 1.5 pt border. `Note:` stays plain text.


**What this is:** Boxes are embedded directly within the delivery script at the moment
they are most relevant. A teacher does not flip to a back page — the box is already
there when needed.

**The distinction that governs box selection:**

> **TIP** = for the teacher's pedagogical approach — teaching/instructional/lesson
> strategy guidance. About how to TEACH this content.
>
> **FEEDBACK** = for responding to student performance — what to say or do when a
> student is stuck, has made an error, has finished early, or the class data requires
> a response right now. About reacting to what STUDENTS have done.

**Four box types — each has a single clear purpose:**

```
Tip: box (§10.5, 0.75 pt, lightbulb icon):
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Tip: [Memorable 2–5 word phrase]                │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  [Teaching/instructional guidance — what the teacher should │
│   do, watch for, or consider pedagogically. About the       │
│   LESSON or the TEACHING APPROACH, not student response.]   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Use for: section-level teaching strategy, T1 Top Tips, any
pedagogical consideration about how to approach this content.
Label: `Tip:` — the bold label word and the icon tell it apart from `Feedback:`.

Feedback: box (§10.5, 0.75 pt, speech-bubble-with-check icon):
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Feedback: — [Short plain-English description]            │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  [How to respond to a student right now. About responding   │
│   to STUDENT PERFORMANCE — errors, stuck moments, early     │
│   finishers, data loop responses.]                          │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Correction-specific format (prefix ):
  LOOKS LIKE: [what the error looks like on their worksheet]
  SAY: "[Exact process question — makes student think]"
  IF THAT DOESN'T WORK: [One more concrete move]
Use for: named mistake correction scripts, "I don't know where
to start" scripts, "I finished early" scripts, data loop
reteach responses, any response to student performance.

Watch: box (§10.5, 1.5 pt border, eye icon):
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Watch: — [Short name of the misconception or error]      │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  [What the error looks like. Why it happens. What to do.]   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Use for: anticipated misconceptions, wrong-example alerts,
specific error patterns the teacher must stay alert for.
Strictly for mistakes — not for strategy or student responses.

Research: box (§10.5, 0.75 pt, book icon):
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Research: — [Short memorable phrase]                     │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  [The finding. Why it matters HERE for THESE students.]     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Use for: non-obvious research findings at section tops where
not knowing the finding would produce worse teaching.
Rare — maximum 1 per section, only where genuinely critical.

NOTE (plain text — no box):
> Note: [Logistics, timing, transitions, brief reminders.]
Use for: everything else. Never promote logistics into a box.
```

**When to use each type — decision rule:**

| Situation | Box type |
|-----------|----------|
| How to approach this section pedagogically | Tip: |
| What strategy to use for this content | Tip: |
| Whole-lesson strategy (T1 Top Tips) | Tip: |
| A student has made a specific error | Feedback: |
| A student says "I don't know where to start" | Feedback: |
| A student finishes early | Feedback: |
| Class data shows most got it wrong — reteach needed | Feedback: |
| ELL student needs language support right now | Feedback: |
| Anticipated misconception — teacher must stay alert | Watch: |
| Wrong example in I Do | Watch: |
| Critical research finding that changes teacher behaviour | Research: |
| Timing note, logistics, transition | Note |

**The quick test:** Ask "Am I responding to something a student has done?" → FEEDBACK.
Ask "Am I guiding the teacher's pedagogical approach?" → LESSON TIP.

**Source:** All box content draws from the **Teaching Strategy Reference Library**
using contextual judgment. Every box must be lesson-specific — no generic advice,
no theory name-dropping.

**Placement rules:**

| Box | Where it belongs |
|-----|-----------------|
| Tip: section teaching strategy | Top of the relevant delivery section |
| Feedback: — named mistake | You Do Step 2, at the start of circulation |
| Feedback: — "I don't know" | We Do, after the Think-Aloud step |
| Feedback: — "I finished early" | You Do, after the ★★★ description |
| Feedback: — data loop reteach | Immediately after each (hollow circle) Fewer than half: data loop branch |
| Watch: — misconception | I Do Step 4 + You Do Step 2 |
| Watch: — error pattern | Start of the section where it typically first appears |
| Research: | Top of I Do, We Do, or You Do — maximum 1 per section |

**Minimum per lesson:** one Feedback: per named T2 mistake + one Feedback: for
"I don't know where to start" + one Feedback: for "I finished early."
No upper limit — generate as many as the lesson warrants.

**Language rules for FEEDBACK boxes:**
- Plain English. Readable aloud mid-lesson. No jargon.
- SAY lines use process questions — not answer hints.
- "If that doesn't work" gives a concrete different move — not "try again."
- Correction SAY lines are answerable with a gesture, a point, or a single word.
- Strategy names shape content but never appear in the teacher-facing text.

**T6 (Answer Key) remains the final teacher page.** No standalone T6b.

## Optional Pedagogical Enhancement Decision Guide

> **V21 changes to this section.** Enhancements 1, 3, 4 and 5 are teacher-side. Enhancement 2 (Variation set): the pupil-printed set is PAUSED (Reason It pages are paused); its oral use stays, and if un-paused it prints as two check boxes `Works` / `Does not work` with no "Why" line. Enhancement 3 (Marzano) pupil box: the word + a drawing zone (`Workspace:` band), never "in my own words". Enhancement 5 is oral.


Choose **maximum 2 per lesson**. Combined time cost must not exceed 6 minutes.
State any selection and time trade-off at the top of the lesson header on T3.

**Decision Framework:** What is the primary challenge — building a new concept,
vocabulary access, fluency, or reasoning? Who is the class — ELL-heavy, SPED,
high-attaining, or mixed?

---

### Optional Enhancement 1: WAGOLL / Co-Constructed Success Criteria

**When to use:** Output quality matters, not just the answer; students
frequently ask "Is this good enough?"
**Skip when:** Procedural fluency lesson; after assessment; most students
sailing through We Do without errors.
**Time cost:** +3–4 min. **Trade:** We Do 8→5 min.
**Where:** After Check Question, before You Do.

Display a completed example (different content from You Do). Students read
silently (30 seconds). Ask: *"What does this have that makes it excellent?
Tell your partner — 20 seconds."* Co-construct max 3 student-generated
criteria. Students write on sheet and self-assess at end of You Do.

---

### Optional Enhancement 2: Variation Theory Expansion Set

**When to use:** Students over/under-generalise; unusual presentations appear
on assessments; concept-boundary lessons.
**Skip when:** Procedural fluency; review; students significantly below grade level.
**Time cost:** +2–3 min. **Trade:** Replace 2–3 You Do problems.

```
★★ VARIATION SET — Which work? Which don't? Why?
A) [One feature changed from the base example]   ✓ or ✗? ___ Why? ___________
B) [Different numbers — same structure]           ✓ or ✗? ___ Why? ___________
C) [Different orientation or context]             ✓ or ✗? ___ Why? ___________
★★★ D) [Multiple things varying at once]
Explain why the answer changes when [feature X] changes: ____________________
```

---

### Optional Enhancement 3: Marzano Multi-Encounter Vocabulary

**When to use:** 3+ high-priority new words; vocabulary is the main barrier
for ELL students; new domain lesson.
**Skip when:** Vocabulary is familiar; only 1–2 new words.
**Time cost:** +3–4 min (encounters 1–3). Encounters 4–6 are embedded free.
**Trade:** Prereq −2 min + We Do −1 min.

| Encounter | When | What happens |
|-----------|------|-------------|
| 1 — Teacher describes it | Opening | Teacher says the word + definition aloud with a visual |
| 2 — Students restate it | I Do | Fill-in-the-blank definition on student sheet |
| 3 — Students draw it | I Do or We Do | Students sketch the word |
| 4 — Students discuss it | We Do | Sentence frame must include the target word |
| 5 — Students sort it | You Do | Sort or analogy using the target word |
| 6 — Students use it in writing | Plenary | Exit reflection requires the target word |

Pupil vocabulary box (3 mm rounded, 0.75 pt black outline, no fill; the word + a drawing zone, never "in my own words"):
```
 VOCABULARY
Word: _______________
In my own words: _______________________________
My drawing:   [Empty space — student sketches here]
```

---

### Optional Enhancement 4: Responsive Scaffolding (Expertise Reversal)

**Time cost:** Zero. Include in almost every lesson.
Explicitly plan the triggers in the Circulation Script.

- **Move a student up:** 3 correct ★★ in a row and quick → say: *"Show me
  without the hints."* Give ★★★ only.
- **Move a student down:** Stuck on ★ after 2 tries → sit with them. Reteach
  at ★ level. Do not advance until secure.
- **Middle-band:** Finished ★ but struggling at ★★ → visit twice. First time:
  Process question. Second time: Self-Regulation question. Still stuck → move
  down trigger.

---

### Optional Enhancement 5: Structured Academic Controversy (★★★ Only)

**When to use:** The ★★★ problem is genuinely ambiguous or counter-intuitive;
the goal is reasoning about WHY; students have enough English to debate.
**Skip when:** ★ or ★★ problems; procedural fluency lesson; another enhancement
already costs 4+ minutes.
**Time cost:** +4–5 min. **Trade:** You Do −2–3 problems + We Do −1 min.

```
★★★ STRUCTURED ACADEMIC CONTROVERSY
Step 1 — Take a position (1 min, alone):    My answer: ___  My reason: ___
Step 2 — Argue your position (1 min):       Partner A tells B. B listens only.
Step 3 — Argue the opposite (1 min):        Partner B argues the other side
                                             even if they disagree.
Step 4 — Agree together (1 min):            Best answer: ___  Because: ___
```
ELL starters: “I think ___ because ___” / *"I argue the opposite: ___
because ___"* / “We agreed ___ because ___”

---

## Optional Enhancement Recommendation Logic

If the user does not specify, apply this logic and state the recommendation
at the top of the lesson header on T3:

- **New concept + ELL-heavy class** → Marzano Vocabulary + Variation Theory
- **New concept + high-attaining class** → Structured Academic Controversy only
- **Fluency practice + mixed ability** → Responsive Scaffolding only (free)
- **Complex word problem lesson** → WAGOLL to set response quality expectations
- **Students frequently ask "Is this right?"** → WAGOLL to build self-assessment
- **Lesson under 50 min** → No optional enhancements
- **Lesson 70+ min** → Up to two optional enhancements

---

## Comprehensive Teacher Wording Reference

These are the exact phrases to copy into the **DO**, **SAY**, and **ASK** labels
in generated delivery pages. All spoken words follow `Say:` / `Ask:`, in curly quotes at 13 pt (§8.16). NOTE boxes appear
where a teacher needs context, not after every entry.

---

**1. STARTING THE LESSON**
DO: Mini whiteboards out before you begin.
SAY: “Mini whiteboards out. Markers ready. 10 seconds per question. No hands up.”

---

**2. COLD CALL (teacher picks — no hands up)**
SAY: “[Name] — what's your answer?”
SAY (boards): “Show me in 3, 2, 1.”

> Note: Scan ALL boards before any student changes their answer. You lose the data the moment students start looking at each other.

---

**3. PARTNER TALK**
DO: Say all three parts before students start: what to discuss, who goes first, how long.
SAY: “Talk to your partner: [specific question]. Partner A starts. [Time]. Go.”
ASK: “Partner B — what did your partner say?”

---

**4. CHECKING THE ROOM**
DO: Walk around. Look at boards and written work. Do not ask "does everyone understand?" — students will say yes even when lost.

> Note: You are looking for a pattern: most students correct, or a specific wrong answer appearing repeatedly. A pattern tells you what to do next.

---

**5. SILENT TEACHER — I Do opening**
DO: Work through the example on the visualiser/board completely. No speaking. Students watch.
DO: Put the marker down. Then begin narrating.
SAY: “Now let me explain what I did and why.”

---

**6. NARRATING THE WORKED EXAMPLE**
SAY: “[Step 1 — plain English]. The reason we do this first is [plain reason].”
SAY: “[Step 2]. Notice [the most important thing about this step].”
DO: Point to each line as you narrate. One short sentence per step.

---

**7. SHOWING A WRONG EXAMPLE**
DO: Write the wrong example clearly. Write the word Wrong beside it.
SAY: “Watch — here is what goes wrong when [plain description of mistake].”
SAY: “See what happens? This is called [name of this mistake in plain English].”
ASK: “What went wrong? Tell your partner. 15 seconds.”

---

**8. SELF-EXPLANATION PAUSE (60 seconds after I Do)**
SAY: “Look at Step [X]. Quietly — why do we do this step before the next one? Write one reason. Mini whiteboard or margin. 60 seconds.”
DO: Walk around. Read what students write. Do not comment.
SAY: “Keep that reason in mind. Let's practise together.”

---

**9. SHOW CALL (displaying student work)**
DO: Place work under the visualiser/board. Ask permission first. Show incorrect work anonymously.
ASK: “What do you notice about this work?”
ASK: “What would you change — and why?”

> Note: Responses should name a specific step, not just "it's right" or "it's wrong." If a student only says "it's wrong," ask: “Which step? Point to it.”

---

**10. SENTENCE FRAME FOR ELL STUDENTS**
DO: Display the sentence frame in a the `Say:` frame.
SAY: “Use the sentence frame. Fill in the blank. You can use the word bank.”

---

**11. WHEN A STUDENT IS STUCK**
DO: Do not give the answer. Point to the specific step where things went wrong.
ASK: “What does this step ask you to do?”
ASK: “What did we do first in the I Do? Can you find that step here?”

> Note: If two questions don't work, draw a simpler version of the problem (smaller numbers, same structure) and try the question again on that version.

---

**12. WHEN WORK IS CORRECT**
SAY: “How would you check that? Try it.”
ASK: “Does that answer make sense?”

---

**13. MOVING A STUDENT UP (3 correct ★★ in a row)**
SAY: “Show me this one without the hints.” [Point to ★★★.]

---

**14. MOVING A STUDENT DOWN (stuck on ★ after 2 tries)**
DO: Sit with them. Work through one example one step at a time.
DO: Let them do each step after you model it. Do not move to ★★ until ★ is secure alone.

---

**15. DATA LOOP — after Check Question**
(hollow circle) Fewer than half correct:
SAY: “Let me show this again — different example.” [Deliver lesson-specific reteach script.]
(half circle) About half correct:
SAY: “A lot of people chose [wrong option]. Here's why that happens.” [Name and correct the mistake. One more mini whiteboard task.]
(solid circle) Most correct:
SAY: “Good. Open your worksheet to the practice section.”

---

**16. RETEACH (quick — completely different example)**
DO: Use different numbers and a different context from the I Do.
SAY: “Let me show you this one more time. Watch.”
DO: Model silently → narrate briefly → one mini whiteboard check.

---

**17. WORD PROBLEM LAUNCH**
DO: Read the problem aloud once at normal speed.
SAY: “Read it to yourself.”
SAY: “Circle the numbers. Underline the question. 30 seconds.”
SAY: “Draw a picture of the problem. Doesn't have to be perfect.”
SAY: “Write your number sentence. Solve it. Fill in the sentence using the word bank.”

---

**18. USING THE WORD BANK**
SAY: “Look at the word bank. Find the word that fits. Use it in your sentence.”
ASK: “Which word best describes what happened in this problem?”

---

**19. CONSOLIDATION QUESTION (word problem debrief)**
ASK: “Why did we [specific step from this problem]? Tell your partner.”
ASK: “Before I tell you — how do you know your answer makes sense?”

---

**20. PROCESS FEEDBACK (when work is wrong)**
DO: Point to the step where the error is. Do not mark it — just point.
ASK: “What were you trying to do at this step?”
ASK: “What does the rule say about [the thing the student got wrong]?”

---

**21. EXIT TICKET LAUNCH**
SAY: “One last question. Think on your own. No talking.”
DO: Wait 45 seconds.
SAY: “Show me in 3, 2, 1.”

---

**22. REFLECTION CLOSE**
SAY: “Fill in the reflection. Use the word bank. Two minutes.”
ASK: “Can you now [I Can statement]? Thumbs up if yes. Flat hand if you need one more day. Both are fine.”

> Note: Note flat hands — check in with those students first tomorrow.

---

**23. CRA BRIDGE (connecting concrete to representational/abstract)**
SAY: “Remember when we used [physical object]? This drawing shows the same thing. Now we write it with numbers.”
DO: Point to the visual model alongside the worked example.

---

**24. ACADEMIC CONTROVERSY LAUNCH (★★★ only)**
SAY: “Take a position. Write your answer and reason. 1 minute alone.”
SAY: “Partner A — tell your position. Partner B, just listen. 1 minute.”
SAY: “Now switch. Partner B argues the opposite — even if you disagree. 1 minute.”
SAY: “Agree on the best answer together. 1 minute.”
ELL starters: “I think ___ because ___” / “I argue the opposite: ___ because ___” / “We agreed ___ because ___”

---

**25. GOAL CHECK (end of lesson)**
SAY: “Read the I Can statement at the top of your sheet.”
ASK: “Can you now [I Can statement]? What's one thing you'd still like to practise?”

---

**26. NO OPT-OUT**
When a student says "I don't know":
DO: Do not move on. Turn to another student.
ASK: “[Name B] — what's the answer?”
DO: After Name B gives the correct answer, return immediately to the first student.
ASK: “[Name A] — what did [Name B] say?”
DO: Name A must say the correct answer aloud. The loop closes only when the original
student gives the right response.

> Note: "I don't know" is a pause, not an exit. This move keeps every student
> in the room accountable for every question — without humiliating anyone, because
> Name A is only asked to repeat what they just heard, not produce an answer from
> scratch. The repetition itself is a low-stakes retrieval act. Use this every time
> a student opts out — the class learns quickly that the question always comes back.

---

**27. SAY IT AGAIN, BETTER**
When a student gives a vague, partial, or vocabulary-free answer:
DO: Acknowledge the idea, then push for precision.
SAY: “That's the right idea. Say it again — use the word [target vocabulary word].”
Or: “Good start. Say it in a full sentence this time.”
DO: Wait. Do not accept the vague version as the final answer.

> Note: This is the single most effective ELL language move in the lesson.
> Vague answers ("it goes up") contain correct reasoning that the student cannot
> yet express precisely. "Say it again, better" forces production of the academic
> language the student needs — while the correct thought is still warm. Never praise
> a partial answer and move on. The student's language grows when they are required
> to refine it, not when they are let off the hook.

---

- **Frayer Model** — Definition, Characteristics, Examples, Non-Examples
  (4-quadrant card)
- **Compare & Contrast** — "What's the same? What's different?"
- **Sorting / Categorising** — Students sort items into groups
- **Semantic Mapping** — Visual web connecting related vocabulary
- **Analogical Reasoning** — "A ray is like a sun ray — starts at the sun,
  goes forever"
- **Superordinate / Subordinate Relationships** — Category hierarchies
- **Marzano Multi-Encounter Sequence** — When vocabulary density is high
  (Optional Enhancement 3)

---

## Gathering Requirements

Before generating materials, ask:

1. **Topic** — Specific concept or skill?
2. **Age/Year Group** — Grade level? (Printed as "Level N" on pupil pages.)
3. **Context** — Curriculum (CCSS, Wisconsin Essential Elements, UK National Curriculum, IB, etc.)?
4. **Student needs** — Default: ELL students and students with special education needs,
   1–3 years below grade level in both mathematics and English. This is the baseline
   assumption for all generated materials. Only override this if the teacher explicitly
   states a different student profile. If a mix is described (some ELL, some not),
   design for the ELL/SPED students — supports do not harm fluent students but their
   absence can shut ELL students out entirely.
5. **Lesson duration** — Default 60 minutes.
6. **Prior knowledge** — What have students already learned? What comes next?
   (Used to write the Schema Connection Hook and to choose the 3–4 Prerequisite Check lessons.)
7. **Output format** — Default **HTML + PDF** (print the HTML at 100 % to PDF). DOCX / PPTX only on
   request, best effort, with Andika embedded.
8. **Uploaded resource** — PDF, image, or pupil worksheet uploaded?
   If yes → follow the **PDF/Image Source Workflow** in full (Part 18). Do NOT skip any step. The
   uploaded material drives the lesson content; this V21 format drives the lesson structure and the
   print design. All V21 requirements still apply in full — the PDF replaces invented content, not the
   lesson framework.
9. **Optional enhancements** — Which (if any)? Apply decision logic if
   unspecified. If using Chunked Flow, limit to one optional enhancement.
10. **CRA stage** — Concept-introduction (begin at Concrete), developing
    (begin at Representational / Pictures), or fluency (begin at Abstract)?
    Default: Representational.
11. **Lesson flow** — After building the Routine Atomisation table (T2), count
    its rows tagged `New`. 3 or fewer → Standard Flow. 4 or more →
    propose two lessons; if the teacher keeps one, Chunked Flow.
    State which flow is in use on T1 and at the top of T3.
12. **Grade Band** — Infer from the grade level provided in item 2. It changes counts and number
    ranges only (Part 16), never type size.
13. **Paper** — A4 (default) or US Letter (**Owner default (2026-10-16, recommended; may change) — Q2**).
14. **Does a MathQuest lesson already exist for this skill?** If the teacher supplies its pupil packet,
    use those pupil pages and build the teacher pages around them (**Owner default (2026-10-16,
    recommended; may change) — Q1**).
15. **Number sense page** — include P1 (default yes, **Owner default — Q4**)?

---
## Grade Band Pupil Material Specification (counts only)

Type size, writing space and slot sizes are the same for every grade: one size L (§8.3) [AUD C-22, LR-10].
The grade band changes only counts, number ranges and sentence length. Pages are age-neutral [P-18].
Teacher pages (T1–T6) are unaffected.

| Element | PK–Grade 2 (ages 4–7) | Grades 3–5 (ages 8–11) |
|---------|-------------------|------------------------|
| Guided cells on the lesson sheet | 2 | 3 |
| Independent cells on the lesson sheet | 3 | 3 |
| Practice per page | 4–6 (one-symbol facts up to 12) | 6 (4 for long procedures); 2 pages |
| Word problem | v1, 1 per page; Kindergarten picture version ≤ 2 per page (frame `__ ○ __ = __`) | v1 (1 per page) or v2 (2 per page) |
| Story sentences | ≤ 10 words; ≤ 3 sentences; active voice only | ≤ 12 words; story lines ≤ 60 characters |
| Fade levels | Guided and Independent for PK–K; the ★★★ last row optional for Grades 1–2 | Full Guided / Independent / last-row range |
| Exit slip | One problem, the `Say:` frame, two check boxes | Same |
| Frames | At most one blank per sentence | At most 2 blanks |

**Writing guidelines for PK–2:**
- Instructions: one action verb per instruction line ("Circle the number." not "Circle the number and underline the question.")
- Word problems: no longer than 3 sentences total
- Avoid multi-part questions on a single line

**Grade band detection rule:** If the teacher specifies a grade (e.g. "Grade 1" or
"Year 2"), apply the matching band automatically without asking. If the grade implies
a split (e.g. "mixed K–1" or "Grade 2–3"), apply the PK–2 counts. If no grade is given,
ask before generating pupil materials. Grades print as "Level N" on pupil pages and only in the footer as
"Grade N" [SC-5].

---
> **V21 changes to this section.** The bullets that encoded the V20 look (class names, pixel sizes, italics, colours, emoji cues, S1–S5 card layouts, the V20 footer and T1 type treatment, warm-up workspace boxes) are deleted; Part 8–10 and the print rules R1–R12 (Part 20) replace them. Every pedagogy bullet is kept, and the ones that mixed pedagogy with the old look are restated at the end of this part.

## Mandatory Rules — Organised by Lesson Phase

> These rules replace the old version-labelled list. Every rule here applies
> to every lesson generated by this prompt. Rules are grouped by when they
> matter most so a teacher or generator can scan the right section quickly.

---

### BEFORE THE LESSON — Page Layout & Planning

**ELL/SPED baseline (applies before any other decision):**
Every lesson is designed for ELL students and students with special education needs
who are 1–3 years below grade level in both mathematics and English. This is not a flag
to set — it is the default. Design every sentence, every instruction, every tip, every
script, every scaffold as if this describes the whole class. See the Presupposed Student
section for the full specification.

**T1 content order (top to bottom):** title → standards line (CCSS · EE · CRA · Flow) → `Lesson guidance:` (≤ 3 boxes) → `Lesson flow:` → `Materials:` → `Vocabulary:`. Layout: §10.9 and PART 0.

**Visual hierarchy rules:**
- Top Tips and Lesson Flow are the two elements a teacher reads in 90 seconds.
  They come first on the page, before materials.
- Materials and vocabulary come last; they are checked, not read.
- Never reorder this structure. Never move tips below the lesson flow.
- Never put section-specific tips (We Do watchouts, circulation notes) on T1 —
  those belong in the Section Tip boxes at the top of T3–T5.

**Page order (never change this):**
- T1 — Lesson at a Glance (ALWAYS first)
- T2 — Atomisation Template
- T2b — Reference Key *(planning reference — print once per unit, keep as a standing
  reference. Not needed during live delivery. A teacher teaching from T3 onward
  does not need T2b at the board. Do not reprint T2b with every lesson.)*
- T3 — Starter + Prerequisite Check + Chaining Bridge
- T4 — I Do + We Do
- T5 — Check Question + You Do (with embedded teaching boxes) + Word Problem + Exit Ticket
- T6 — Answer Key (ALWAYS last)

**Page targets (expand freely — never squeeze):**
- Teacher: aim for 7 pages. T1 always first. T6 always last. No standalone T6b.
  Teaching boxes are embedded within T3–T5 at the moment they are relevant.
- Pupil packet: P0–P6 (§9). Expand if needed. No answer key in student materials.

**T1 — Lesson at a Glance (MANDATORY content):**
- Tips must be lesson-specific — generic advice is not acceptable at either tier.
- NEVER leave blank workspace, empty boxes, or answer lines on any teacher page.
- Board-space references appear as text: `[BOARD SPACE — Teacher models here]`

**Atomisation Template — T2 (MANDATORY structure):**
- Section 1 — Conceptual Atomisation: Category, Comparative, Fact, and
  Conceptual atoms ONLY. Task and Routine atoms do NOT go here — they belong
  in Section 2. (Reason: Section 1 is for knowledge and understanding;
  Section 2 is for procedures and steps.)
- Section 2 — Routine Atomisation: Task and Routine atoms, each numbered
  sequentially (1, 2, 3…). Every row = one single action starting with a verb.
  ALWAYS mark the Critical Atom [Critical Atom] — the one atom that, if a student
  understands it, determines success for the whole routine.
- Section 3 — How to Teach: Teaching steps with memorable verb names. Each
  teaching step names which student steps it covers. Include a chaining note
  when 3+ student steps are combined into one teaching moment.
- Section 4 — Common Mistakes: short ALL-CAPS names, no letter codes.
  "Broken Step" column names the student step number where the mistake occurs.
  EVERY named mistake must match one wrong answer in the Check Question.
- ALWAYS identify all six atom types using full names:
  Category / Comparative / Fact / Task / Routine / Conceptual.

### DURING THE LESSON — Delivery Rules

**All teacher scripts (T3–T5):**
- CHECK is not a separate label — what to look for folds into the NOTE box.
- Every section uses numbered steps. The teacher never wonders what comes
  next — the answer is always the next number.
- Plain language only. No jargon. Explain maths as if to someone who is rusty.
- Data loop labels: "fewer than half" / "about half" / "most" — never percentages.
- NOTE boxes appear only at genuine decision points — not after every step.

**No hands up — ever:**
- Cold Call, mini whiteboards (MWBs), and structured protocols only.
- "3-2-1 Show Me!" for mini whiteboards. Scan all boards before confirming.

**I Do — Silent Teacher:**
- ALWAYS open with the Schema Connection Hook (Step 0): two sentences naming the prior
  skill this lesson extends and the next skill it unlocks. Specific, not vague.
- Demonstrate FIRST (no speaking). Narrate SECOND (short explanation).
- Show wrong example immediately after first correct example. Name it in plain English.
- ALWAYS pair worked example with a visual model (side-by-side or stacked —
  never on a separate page). See Math Visual & Rendering Specification below.
- Self-Explanation (60–90 seconds): private WHY prompt after narration.
  Students write on MWBs or in margin — NEVER in a printed box on student sheet.
- For spatial, geometric, or graphical topics: Example 2 MUST vary orientation or
  presentation, not just numbers. See Orientation Variation rule above.
- If the Routine table has 4 or more rows tagged New: propose two lessons, else Chunked Flow. State which flow is used at the top of T3.

**We Do — Guided Practice:**
- Partner talk: tell students WHAT to discuss, WHO speaks first, HOW LONG, then say go.
- Follow with: "Partner B — what did your partner say?"
- ALWAYS include a Student Think-Aloud moment on Task 2: students whisper steps aloud
  to themselves as they solve. Teacher listens — not to boards, to language.
- Apply No Opt-Out whenever a student says "I don't know." Apply Say It Again Better
  whenever a student gives a vague answer. Both scripts are in the Wording Reference.
- Check that most students are getting mini whiteboard tasks correct before moving to
  Check Question. If many boards show errors, do one more task before moving on.

**Check Question:**
- One multiple-choice question. Each wrong answer matches a named mistake from T2.
- Deliver on mini whiteboards: "3-2-1 Show Me!" Scan all boards first.
- Data-Driven Loop — three colour-coded columns:
  (hollow circle) Fewer than half correct → reteach (different example, verbatim script written here)
  (half circle) About half correct → fix the errors (address the mistake pattern)
  (solid circle) Most correct → move on

**You Do — Independent Practice:**
- Practice pages hold the §8.12.1 count (revised OR-b), 1–2 pages, not counting the word problem.
  If the source PDF has more than 10 practice problems, use all of them — there is no
  upper limit when problems come from a source worksheet.
- **Fade (§8.12.5):** ★ = Guided cells (cell 1 has step 1 in grey; hint scaffolds grey); ★★ = Independent
  (structure only); ★★★ = the last Practice row of edge cases in the same format.
- **ALWAYS paper-based.** You Do problems must be on the generated student worksheet or
  on a referenced printed sheet (Method E). Never delivered verbally or on mini whiteboards.
- Circulation Script: ELL and SPED students first within 60 seconds.
  Feedback at Process or Self-Regulation level only — never give the answer.
- Responsive Scaffolding: 3 correct ★★ in a row → move to the ★★★ row.
  Stuck on ★ after 2 tries → sit with student, reteach at ★ level.
- Peer Tutoring trigger: when a student is secure at ★★ (3+ correct) AND another student
  is slow but not confused at ★, pair them briefly — one specific step, teacher-scripted,
  teacher-checked within 2 minutes. Never replace your own visit with a peer tutor.
- Practice pages follow §9.5: one ruled grid, the step strip on top, the edge cases (★★★) in the last row,
  in the same cell format. No cards, no dashed boxes, no capital labels.

**Word Problem (mandatory every lesson):**
- Single ★★ version only. Sentence frame and word bank are the primary
  scaffolds — not difficulty reduction.
- Include verbatim Consolidation Script: one WHY question, one expected
  response, one self-regulation follow-up.

**Exit Ticket / Plenary:**
- One problem on mini whiteboards. Show Call: one correct board + one
  common-mistake board. Critique at Process level. Anonymous.
- Exit slip P6: one problem, the `Say:` frame and two check boxes; no written reflection.
- Include verbatim Reflection Consolidation Script.

---

### ALWAYS / NEVER — Absolute Rules

**ALWAYS:**
- Double-check all mathematical accuracy before outputting.
- Say "visualiser/board" — not "visualiser" alone.
- Use full terms over abbreviations; abbreviate only after first use on a
  page or inside a table cell where space is tight.
- Use all six atom types in every lesson — do not drop any type. Atom type names are:
  Category / Comparative / Fact / Task / Routine / Conceptual. Never use "Transformation"
  as an atom type — it is an older term for "Task." All generated output must use "Task."
- Apply the fade of §8.12.5 to You Do: ★ Guided (step 1 grey in cell 1, hints grey), ★★ Independent
  (structure only), ★★★ last-row edge cases. Structural scaffolds stay at every level.
- Include the Schema Connection Hook (Step 0) at the start of every I Do — name the prior
  learning this lesson extends and the next learning it unlocks. Two sentences maximum.
- Follow the 6-step I Do structure (Steps 0–5) exactly as specified: Schema Hook →
  Silent Demo + Visual Model → Narration → Example 2 + Visual Model → Wrong Example →
  Self-Explanation + Transition. Do not add or reorder steps.
- Include the Chaining Bridge (Section ②b) for any routine with 3+ atoms.
- Apply the Prereq Check Data Loop ((hollow circle) Fewer than half:(half circle) About half:(solid circle) Most:) after every Prereq Check task.
- Apply Orientation/Presentation Variation to the second I Do worked example for all
  spatial, geometric, or graphical topics.
- Count new steps in the Routine Atomisation table. If 4 or more are new: use
  Chunked Flow (I Do Part 1 → We Do Part 1 → I Do Part 2 → We Do Part 2) and state
  which flow is used at the top of T3.
- TIP is strictly for pedagogical/teaching guidance. FEEDBACK is strictly for
  responding to student performance. Never use TIP where the content is responding
  to what a student has done — use FEEDBACK.
- Embed boxes inline: Tip: for section teaching strategy, Feedback: for correction
  scripts, Feedback: for stuck/finished/reteach responses, Watch: for misconceptions,
  Research: where a critical finding governs delivery.
  Minimum per lesson: one Feedback: per T2 mistake + one  "I don't know" FEEDBACK
  + one  "I finished early" FEEDBACK. No upper limit.
- Include the Schema Extension script (You Do Step 6) for early finishers who complete
  ★★★: one of three options — create a variation / test the method / explain the rule.
- Write every student-facing instruction with: (a) one action verb per line, and
  (b) maximum 8 words per line. If a step requires more words, split it or deliver it
  verbally — do not print it as a multi-action sentence.
- Include a Manipulative reference in the teacher page at every You Do ★ section,
  naming the specific physical tool available for any student who needs to build the
  concept concretely before working abstractly. Frame it as universal availability,
  not as an accommodation flag. Include the verbal prompt: "These are here if you need them."
- Label T2b explicitly as "Planning reference — print once per unit, not with each
  lesson." T2b is not a live delivery document.
- All DO, SAY, ASK, and NOTE scripts must embody strategies from the Teaching Strategy
  Reference Library. DO steps reflect Specific Observable Directions, Prioritized
  Circulation, Habits and Routines. SAY scripts reflect Economy of Language, Wait Time,
  Warm Demander. ASK scripts reflect Cold Call, Elaborative Interrogation, Stretch It.
  NOTE lines reflect Plan for Error, Culture of Error, Expertise Reversal.
- Every tip (T1 and Section Tips) must begin with a memorable 2–5 word phrase as its
  bold title. Generic category labels are banned at both tiers.
- Apply the ELL/SPED Presupposed Student baseline to every design decision. Every SAY
  script uses sentences under 12 words. Every ASK script offers a point/gesture
  alternative. Every correction tip gives a question answerable without full-sentence
  English production. Every word problem is linguistically simple and culturally accessible.
- State CCSS as abbreviated code only on T1 (e.g. `3.NBT.A.2`). Full standard text
  belongs in the T3 lesson header only.
- Use full plain-English atom type names throughout: Category, Comparative, Fact, Task,
  Routine, Conceptual. No abbreviations (Cat/Com/F/Con/T/R) anywhere in generated output.
- Bold the key concept name at the start of every Skill or Concept cell in T2 Section 1,
  followed by a colon: **Decompose:** [sentence]. **Tens Multiplication:** [sentence].
- Bold the first action verb in every student step in T2 Section 2 and T2b:
  **Decompose** both numbers. **Draw** a 2×2 grid. **Multiply** the top-left box. **Add** all parts.
- Bold abbreviation terms in T2b abbreviations table: **MWBs**, **ELL/SPED**, **RCDWS**, etc.
- Include at least one counting sequence (Category 1) as Q1 in every Number Sense Warm-Up.
  Vary the interval and format each session (count by 7s, count back by 25s, fractions, etc.).
- Write Spaced Retrieval Q6 word problems as two separate sentences on separate lines.
  Never run money amounts, units, or numbers directly into adjacent words without spacing.
- Label all LESSON TIP boxes with the prefix "Tip:" (not "TIP" alone) so teachers
  immediately know the box contains pedagogical guidance, not a student response.

**NEVER:**
- Render pedagogical guidance blocks in generated lesson output.
- Generate a QA Checklist page or a standalone T6b Common Responses page.
- Include Full Chain, Sub-Chain, or Variation Sequence tables as standalone output.
- Cut off or squeeze content to hit a page target — add pages as needed.
- Use percentage thresholds in data loop labels — use plain English only.
- Print a ★ cell without its grey step-1 trace and grey hint scaffolds, or print hints on ★★ cells.
- Use "Transformation" as an atom type label anywhere in generated output.
- Use atom type abbreviations (Cat, Com, F, Con, T, R) anywhere in generated output.
- Use generic category labels as tip titles at either tier ("The hardest moment:", etc.).
- Put more than 3 tips on T1 — section-specific tips belong in T3–T5 Section Tip boxes.
- Compress card workspace to avoid a page break — add the extra page.
- Use theory jargon inside tip or watch boxes — strategy names shape the content but
  never appear in the teacher-facing text itself.
- Write a SAY script sentence longer than 12 words.
- Write an ASK script that requires full-sentence English production — always provide
  a gesture, point, or single-word alternative alongside it.
- Write a correction script that asks "Why did you...?" — use process prompts instead:
  "Point to...", "Which step...", "Show me..."
- Assume a student understands the question just because they understand the mathematics.
  Language and mathematical barriers are separate — both must be addressed.
- Write a student-facing instruction with more than one action verb per line.
- Write a student-facing instruction longer than 8 words per line.
- Use more than four box types in delivery pages — only TIP, FEEDBACK, WATCH, RESEARCH,
  and plain NOTE. Inventing a fifth box type is a violation of the formatting system.
- Use a FEEDBACK box for teaching strategy guidance — that is TIP.
  FEEDBACK is for responding to student performance only.
- Use a WATCH box for teaching guidance or student responses — WATCH is strictly for
  misconception and error alerts that the teacher must stay vigilant for.
- Use a TIP or FEEDBACK box for logistics or timing — those are plain `> Note:` lines.
- Deviate from the 6-step I Do structure (Steps 0–5). Do not re-split into 8 steps,
  do not merge further, do not swap the order of steps.
- Reprint T2b with every lesson — it is a planning reference, printed once per unit.
- Omit the counting sequence (Category 1) from any Number Sense Warm-Up — Q1 is always a
  counting/skip-counting question. This is mandatory, not optional.
- Skip bolding the first action verb in T2 Section 2 student step descriptions —
  **Decompose**, **Draw**, **Multiply**, **Add** etc. must be bold in every step row.
- Run Q6 word problem text without sentence breaks — always write as separate
  properly spaced sentences, never as a run-on or with symbols crashing into words.

---

### Rules restated in V21 form (they replace the deleted V20 bullets that mixed pedagogy with the old look)

**ALWAYS:**
- Put at most 3 boxes on T1 in the `Lesson guidance:` band (`Tip:` / `Watch:`): whole-lesson strategic level
  only — Critical Atom, ELL vocabulary barrier, optional research rationale. Section-specific tips go at the
  top of the delivery section where something genuinely useful can be said.
- State the CRA stage as a plain line `CRA stage: <stage>`; include the CRA bridge script if Representational
  or Abstract.
- Give the ELL oral frames as `Say:` lines in the teacher script; pupil pages carry only the `Say:` band.
- Use only four box types in delivery pages: `Tip:`, `Feedback:`, `Watch:`, `Research:` (§10.5); `Note:` is
  plain text. Minimum per lesson: one Feedback per T2 mistake, one Feedback for "I don't know", one for
  "I finished early".
- Keep the T1 order: title → standards line → Lesson guidance → Lesson flow → Materials → Vocabulary.
- Give inline expected answers in the key colour #C2410C bold (Part 4).
- Detect the grade band from the grade given (Part 16); it changes counts and number ranges only.
- Add a `Research:` box at the top of I Do, We Do or You Do only where a non-obvious finding directly
  governs what the teacher must do for ELL/SPED pupils; at most one per section.
- Write atom types, teaching step names and mistake names in bold black sentence case in T2 and T2b.
- Show spaced-retrieval domain labels in brackets, plain 11 pt, on the teacher page.
- Keep stacked paired equations aligned on their `=` (one grid, the `=` and the slots in columns).

**NEVER:**
- Print a self-explanation box, a reflection sentence or any composed-writing element on a pupil page.
- Print completed frame answers or expected responses on a pupil page. The anchor chart's worked states and
  its filled `Say:` frame are the only completed responses, by design; Guided cell 1 shows step 1 in grey.
- Use a Tip box for responses to pupil performance (that is Feedback), or a Feedback box for teaching
  guidance (that is Tip). Label a Tip box anything but `Tip:`.
- Place Materials or Vocabulary above Lesson guidance or Lesson flow on T1.
- Put more problems on a page than §8.12.1 allows, or fewer than the page can hold at size L.
- Print the Check Question, an error-analysis item or a multiple-choice item on a pupil page.

### OPTIONAL ENHANCEMENTS (max 2 per lesson)

> **V21 changes to this section.** Variation Theory: the pupil-printed set is paused; oral use only.


| Enhancement | Use when | Skip when | Time cost |
|-------------|----------|-----------|-----------|
| WAGOLL | Output quality matters; students ask "is this good enough?" | Fluency lesson; most students sailing through We Do | +3–4 min (cut We Do by 3 min) |
| Variation Theory Expansion Set | Students over/under-generalise across problem *types*; concept-boundary lesson. Note: Orientation Variation (changing the visual presentation of the same concept) is now mandatory in the I Do for spatial topics and does not require this enhancement — this enhancement adds a dedicated student *practice set* that systematically varies one feature at a time across multiple problems. | Fluency/review; students below grade level | +2–3 min (replace 2 You Do problems) |
| Marzano Vocabulary | 3+ new key words; vocabulary is the main ELL barrier | Only 1–2 new words; vocabulary already familiar | +3–4 min (cut Prereq 2 min + We Do 1 min) |
| Responsive Scaffolding | Almost always — no time cost | — | 0 min |
| Academic Controversy | ★★★ problem is genuinely ambiguous; students can debate in English | ★ or ★★ lesson; another enhancement costs 4+ min | +4–5 min (cut You Do 2–3 problems) |

**When in doubt:** Over-atomise rather than under-atomise. Over-script rather
than under-script. Pair every procedure with a visual.

## Part 19 — Maths on the page: Andika notation and the drawing rules (MANDATORY)

### 19.1 Notation — Andika HTML, no maths library [TY-1…7, AUD C-15]

- Digits and operators are Andika text: `+`, `−` (U+2212), `×` (U+00D7), `÷`, `=`, `<`, `>`. Never a
  hyphen for minus, never the letter x for times, never `*` or `/`.
- Horizontal equations: one flex row at 28 pt, operators bold and centred in a 0.8 em box, gaps 0.28 em,
  slots per §8.10 (`.eq` in Appendix A §9).
- Vertical facts: a 3-track grid (track 0.72 em; track 1 is the operator track), digits right-aligned,
  a 1.5 pt sum rule, a 12 mm open answer row (`factStack` in Appendix D).
- Column arithmetic with regrouping: tracks 0.95 em, `T O` heads at 12 pt bold, an 8 mm regroup strip
  over the column it regroups into, a 12 mm digit strip for the answer (`columnStack` in Appendix D).
- Fractions: always stacked — numerator over a 1.5 pt bar over denominator, fraction digits 20 pt; the
  whole part of a mixed number stays 28 pt. HTML: `<span class="frac"><span>3</span><span>4</span></span>`
  styled as an inline column with a 1.5 pt border between (or an SVG with the same geometry).
- Chat-only answers (no HTML): plain Unicode (×, ÷, −, ½).

### 19.2 Drawing rules for every visual [WDS 11.1, RP-1…8]

- **Inline SVG only**, viewBox in millimetres equal to the drawn size; never PNG, never clip-art, never
  decoration. One builder per representation (Appendix D).
- **Never draw the answer.** No count label on a set to be counted; no hands on a draw-the-hands clock;
  number-line labels omitted at the answer's tick; partition lines absent when partitioning is the task.
  The only completed responses drawn are in Model / anchor-chart states (grey or black) and on keys
  (key colour).
- Minimum sizes at L (below) are floors. A visual may grow to 1.25× its minimum and is never distorted;
  it is the same size in every cell of a section (OR-c). Exception: a picture the pupil works on grows to
  fill its cell; the anchor chart enlarges (§8.11).
- Labels inside a visual: Andika at 12 pt (zone size); printed targets at 28 pt.
- Strokes from §8.5 only. Shaded = flat #949494. Two sets = solid vs hollow. Grey only for shaded parts,
  traces and faded hints.
- Any part a pupil shades, counts by touching or writes in is at least 6 mm in its smaller dimension.
- Bar lengths and box counts never encode the numbers (schematic, constant across a section).
- Place the visual beside or above the example it belongs to, inside the same cell.

### 19.3 Minimum sizes at L [WDS 11.2]

| Representation (Appendix D function) | Minimum at L | Max columns at L |
|---|---|---|
| Ten frame `tenFrame` | 50 × 20 mm (cell 10 mm; 11 mm when the pupil draws counters) | 3 |
| Counters / pictures `counters` | item 12 mm, pitch 15 mm, rows of ≤ 5 | by fit |
| Dot tile `dotTile` | 9 mm square, dots 2 mm | — |
| Base-ten, up to 99 `base10` | unit 3.5 mm; 50.5 × 35 mm | 3 |
| Base-ten, 100–299 | unit 3.4 mm; 85.4 × 70 mm | 2 |
| Quick-draw `quickDraw` | square 8 mm, stick 10 mm, dot 2.4 mm, all 1.5 pt | — |
| Place-value chart `pvChart` | column 17 mm × row 12 mm; head row 8 mm | by columns |
| Number line `numberLine` | tick pitch ≥ 8 mm; length ≥ 160 mm; whole ticks 5 mm at 1.5 pt | 1 |
| Number track | boxes 18 × 16 mm (≥ 10.5 mm pitch), 3 mm rounded, 1.5 pt outline | 1 |
| Number bond `numberBond` | boxes 20 mm | 2 |
| Bar model `barModel` | bar 14 mm tall; length ≥ 42 mm | 1 (2 in v2) |
| Array `arrayDots` (≤ 5 × 5) | pitch 9 mm; 45 mm | 3 |
| Array (≤ 10 × 10) | pitch 8 mm, 1.5 mm gap after 5; 81.5 mm | 2 |
| Equal groups `equalGroups` | group outline 30 × 22 mm | by fit |
| Fraction circle `fracCircle` | diameter 50 mm | 2 |
| Fraction bar `fracBar` | 56 × 18 mm | 2 |
| Hundredths grid `hundredths` | side 45 mm | 2 |
| Analog clock `clock` (hour, half hour, 5 min) | diameter 50 mm (38 mm in half-width panels) | 3 |
| Analog clock (1 min; draw the hands) | 52 mm | 3 |
| Hop track `hopTrack` | pitch 11 mm, numbers 15 pt bold | — |
| Column subtraction with regroup strip `columnStackSub` | tracks 0.95 em at 28 pt (9.4 mm); regroup strip 8 mm; answer strip 12 mm; about 30 × 52 mm (+ 9 mm Check line) | 3 (2 with the Check line) |
| 2D shape `shape` (judge, name, partition) | bounding box 42 mm | 3 |
| Angle (arm length) | 35 mm | 3 |
| Coordinate grid (cell) | 8 mm | 1–2 |
| Generic coins | diameters 17.5 / 19.75 / 22.0 / 24.26 mm for 1 / 5 / 10 / 25 (scale 1.0) | 2 |
| Ruler | true scale only (print at 100 %) | 1 |
| Digital clock | 48 × 19 mm | 3 |
| Clock with outer minute ring (Model) | 65 mm | 2 |
| Tenths strip | 70 × 16 mm | 2 |
| Area model (cell) | 25 × 14 mm | 1 |
| Base-ten, 300 and over | unit 2.5 mm, flats 5 per row: 174.5 × 52 mm | 1 |
| Graph (plot area) | 100 × 80 mm | 1 |
| Tally box | 32 × 12 mm | — |

### 19.4 Worked example format [BD-4, LR-1, AUD §2]

The worked example lives on the **anchor chart** (§9.3): each step is a panel showing the item in the
state that step produces, the step's numeral, icon and name beside the drawing (Steps take 40–45 % of the
width; when that column would be under 55 mm, the steps go underneath instead). The lesson sheet's
`Guided Practice:` band repeats the Steps beside the cells. The teacher's I Do script refers to the same
step numbers.

### 19.5 Platform notes — Claude vs. Gemini

| Platform | HTML output | Chat/markdown output |
|---|---|---|
| **Claude (claude.ai)** | Andika embedded or self-hosted (Part 8.2); no maths library needed. | Unicode symbols (×, ÷, −, ½) |
| **Google Gemini** | Same. Do not add KaTeX / MathJax. | `$...$` may render in Gemini chat; prefer Unicode |
| **Both — HTML output** | One HTML file with the Appendix A stylesheet, inline SVG from Appendix D, then print to PDF at 100 % (A4). | — |
